  - *Evidence:* docs/getting-started.md:53 says "Put keys in `.env` at the project root, or export them in your shell." src/compactbench/config.py:14-19 configures `Settings` with `env_file=".env"`, but every provider bypasses `Settings` and reads the process environment directly: providers/groq.py:40, providers/anthropic.py:41, providers/openai.py:40, providers/ollama.py:41 all use `os.environ.get(...)`. Grepping for `settings` across src/ returns only a URL in a comment (runner/costs.py:30) — the `Settings` object is imported nowhere. Verified empirically: in a directory with `.env` containing `COMPACTBENCH_GROQ_API_KEY=…`, `settings.groq_api_key` resolves to the value while `GroqProvider()` raises `ProviderError: Groq API key required. Set COMPACTBENCH_GROQ_API_KEY or pass api_key=.`
  - *Why:* Half the documented ways to configure a key are dead, and the failure mode is a confusing "key required" error while a `.env` file with the key sits right there. This is the step immediately before a user's first real run — the highest-drop-off point in the funnel.
  - *Fix:* Pick one and make it true. Cheapest correct fix: have `compactbench/config.py` call `load_dotenv()`-equivalent behaviour by pushing resolved `Settings` values into `os.environ` at import, or better, have each provider fall back to `settings.<provider>_api_key` and add the missing `anthropic_api_key` / `openai_api_key` fields to `Settings` (config.py:22-24 currently only has groq/google/ollama). Either way, add a test asserting a `.env`-only key reaches the provider. If you'd rather not support `.env`, delete the claim at getting-started.md:53 and the `env_file` setting.
- **[P1 · STALE · S]** "Three families / 15 templates" is wrong in 8 files — there are four families and 20 elite_practice templates
  - *Evidence:* PR #27 (b7256d2, 2026-04-19) added the `reference_resolution` family. `compactbench suites list` now reports elite_practice = 20 templates across 4 families, starter = 4 templates across 4 families. Stale claims: README.md:97 ("15 public Elite practice templates + 15 hidden ranked templates across three launch families"), docs/elite-program.md:36-46, docs/faq.md:39-44 ("v1 ships three families"), docs/governance/elite-versions.md:13-19 and 86-92, docs/methodology.md:129, docs/getting-started.md:41, docs/architecture/workorders.md:233-259, notebooks/try_compactbench.ipynb cell 7 ("3 templates x 1 case each"). The counts also contradict each other: README.md:97 says 15 hidden, elite-program.md:46 and elite-versions.md:19 say 20 per family / 60 hidden.
  - *Why:* A benchmark's central claim is its content inventory. Getting it wrong in eight places — and contradicting yourself about the size of the hidden set in two of them — is the fastest way to lose a technically literate reader, which is the entire audience. It also means the fourth family, one of the project's few post-launch wins, is invisible to anyone reading the docs.
  - *Fix:* Fix all eight sites to 4 families / 20 public practice templates, and settle the hidden-set number once (README.md:97 vs elite-program.md:46) — state it in exactly one place (docs/governance/elite-versions.md) and link there from the others. Add `reference_resolution` to the family tables at elite-program.md:40-44, faq.md:41-43, elite-versions.md:13-17, and to the sentence at methodology.md:129. Leave CHANGELOG.md:14-46 alone — that entry is historically correct for 0.1.0.
- **[P1 · STALE · M]** CONTRIBUTING.md's only "pick something up" section points at three closed issues; the repo has zero open help-wanted issues
  - *Evidence:* CONTRIBUTING.md:15-23 — "## Open workorders — pickup welcome / Three planned workorders … are open as GitHub issues and labeled `help wanted`" — links #5, #6 ("good starter issue"), #7. Via the GitHub API all three are CLOSED (WO-008 leaderboard, WO-009 Elite templates, WO-010 docs/PyPI release, all closed after v0.1.0 shipped). The repo has 4 issues total, ever: those three plus #38. The same dead links are repeated at docs/leaderboard.md:106-110 ("## Tracking") and docs/faq.md:77 ("Method submission questions: comment on #5").
  - *Why:* The project is getting outside interest right now (#37 from jaaabir, #38 from alexander-potemkin). Someone who wants to help reads CONTRIBUTING, clicks the "good starter issue", lands on a closed 4-month-old issue, and leaves. There is nothing else for them to pick up — no open `help wanted` or `good first issue` exists.
  - *Fix:* Delete CONTRIBUTING.md:15-23 and replace it with 5-8 genuinely open, scoped issues labeled `good first issue` / `help wanted`. Several fall straight out of this review: fix the broken doc commands, add the doc-command test, publish baselines, add `compactbench methods list`, extra_body passthrough for vLLM reasoning models (flagged by jaaabir on #37), CrewAI/Haystack adapters (docs/integrations.md:206 already invites these). Fix docs/leaderboard.md:106-110 and docs/faq.md:77 to point at the live issue tracker rather than closed issues.
- **[P1 · STALE · S]** Anthropic and OpenAI providers ship but are absent from the README, blank in `providers list`, and listed as "deferred" in the architecture doc
  - *Evidence:* Both providers are registered (src/compactbench/providers/__init__.py:21-28) since PR #19. But: README.md:35 says only "Swap `--provider ollama` for `groq` or `google-ai-studio`"; README.md:93 lists "real providers (Groq / Google AI Studio / Ollama)". `compactbench providers list` prints `anthropic` and `openai` rows with an EMPTY Notes column, because the hand-maintained dict at cli.py:220-226 was never updated — so the CLI tells you the provider exists but not which env var it needs. docs/architecture/decisions.md:181 (D7) still says "Anthropic Claude, OpenAI GPT … integration when a funding path opens" and Part G:244 lists "Paid model providers (Anthropic / OpenAI …)" as deferred. submissions/_template/config.yaml:10 restricts `provider` to "groq, google-ai-studio, ollama, mock". Only docs/getting-started.md:31-38 documents them correctly.
  - *Why:* Anthropic and OpenAI are the providers most readers already have keys for, and getting-started.md:41 explicitly recommends them for real runs because the Groq free tier can't cover a full suite. The README — the page most people read — hides them, `providers list` gives no config hint, and a submitter reading config.yaml believes they aren't allowed. That is the difference between "I can try this in two minutes" and "I need to sign up for Groq first."
  - *Fix:* Update README.md:35 and :93 to name all five real providers; fill in the `notes` dict at cli.py:220-226 for `anthropic` and `openai` (or better, move the notes onto the Provider classes so the registry is the single source of truth and can't drift again); update the allowed-provider comment at submissions/_template/config.yaml:10; and add a revision-history row to decisions.md:270-272 marking D7 superseded rather than leaving the canonical architecture doc asserting the opposite of the code.
- **[P1 · STALE · S]** Documented run sizes are wrong by 2.4x, and the "30-second try" is a 225-call run
  - *Evidence:* docs/getting-started.md:41 claims "15 templates × default 5 cases × 2 drift cycles × ~3 eval items per cycle = ~450 LLM calls" for elite_practice. The tool's own projector disagrees: `compactbench run --method built-in:hybrid-ledger --suite elite_practice --provider groq --model llama-3.3-70b-versatile --estimate` reports **100 cases, 1080 API calls, 486,888 tokens, ~$0.35** — the hand-math uses the stale 15-template count and ignores the compaction and continuation calls per cycle. Likewise README.md:22 heads the section "## 30-second try" and docs/index.md:25 promises "**3 minutes with Ollama**"; `--estimate` for that exact command (`--suite starter --provider ollama --model llama3.2`) reports **20 cases / 225 API calls**, which on a local llama3.2 is tens of minutes, not 30 seconds.
  - *Why:* Users size their quota and their patience off these numbers. Someone who trusts "~450 calls" and starts a Groq run burns through the free tier mid-run; someone who trusts "30-second try" abandons a run they think has hung. Both leave believing the tool is broken rather than that the docs are stale.
  - *Fix:* Delete the hand-arithmetic at getting-started.md:41 and replace it with the actual command — `compactbench run --suite elite_practice … --estimate` — plus its current output, and add a "size your run first" step to the getting-started flow before "Run your first benchmark" (`--estimate` is currently only mentioned in passing inside a caching admonition at line 51). Retitle README.md:22 to something honest ("## Try it") and either add `--case-count 1 --drift-cycles 1` to the README.md:30-33 snippet so it really is fast, or state the real call count. Fix docs/index.md:25.
- **[P1 · RISK · S]** The mock-provider iteration loop the docs recommend produces meaningless scores with no warning
  - *Evidence:* docs/writing-a-compactor.md:62-74 — "## Testing locally / Use the mock provider for reproducible local iteration" — shows `--provider mock` and says "Then inspect `results.jsonl`", with no caveat. src/compactbench/providers/mock.py:26-33 defaults `default: str = ""`, and the CLI constructs it with no arguments, so **every** eval answer is the empty string. I ran the docs' own compactor example this way against `starter`: `overall_score 0.375`, `locked_decision_retention` mean 0.000 over 30 items, `entity_integrity` 0.000 over 60, but `planning_soundness` 1.000 and `drift_resistance` 1.000 — all artifacts of scoring "". docs/getting-started.md:38 does warn ("scores are non-interpretable") but also mis-describes the behaviour as "canned 'hello' responses"; writing-a-compactor.md, the page that actually tells you to do it, warns nothing.
  - *Why:* A new contributor follows writing-a-compactor.md, writes a correct compactor, sees 0.000 on the two highest-weighted item types and a 0.375 overall, and concludes their method is broken. The one loop the docs offer for iterating without an API key actively misleads. Perfect drift_resistance of 1.000 on empty responses is also the kind of number that ends up screenshotted somewhere.
  - *Fix:* Add an admonition to docs/writing-a-compactor.md:62-74: mock returns an empty string for every call, so only pipeline mechanics (artifact validates, all cycles complete, compression ratio) are meaningful — item scores are not. Fix the wording at docs/getting-started.md:38 to match mock.py:26-33. Consider giving the CLI-constructed MockProvider a `default` that at least echoes something, so the pipeline smoke is less deceptive.
- **[P1 · GAP · S]** Integrations are promised end-to-end but there is no runnable path from the CLI
  - *Evidence:* README.md:37 — "Already have production code in LangChain or LlamaIndex? Wrap it with `compactbench.integrations` … and benchmark what you're already running." docs/integrations.md:55 then dead-ends: "Hand `compactor` to `compactbench run --method …` (via a custom wrapper) or use the runner API directly" — with no wrapper shown and no runner-API example anywhere in the docs. It cannot work as written: runner/_resolver.py:14-57 resolves `--method path.py:ClassName` to a *class*, and runner/run.py:75 + the `Compactor.__init__(self, provider, model)` contract at compactors/base.py:23-25 mean the CLI constructs it with exactly `(provider, model)`. `LangChainCompactor.__init__` (integrations/langchain.py:271-280) requires a keyword-only `compaction_fn`, so `--method …:LangChainCompactor` can never be instantiated.
  - *Why:* "Benchmark the code you already run" is the strongest adoption pitch the project has, and it is the one path that has no working example. Every reader who follows it hits the phrase "via a custom wrapper" and has to reverse-engineer the resolver to get anywhere.
  - *Fix:* Replace "via a custom wrapper" at docs/integrations.md:55 with the actual 10-line subclass, e.g. `class MySummaryCompactor(LangChainCompactor): def __init__(self, provider, model): super().__init__(provider, model, compaction_fn=summarise, method_name="my-summary")`, followed by the exact `compactbench run --method my_method.py:MySummaryCompactor …` invocation. Add the same for LlamaIndex at line 168. Add an integration test that runs one such file through the CLI with `--provider mock` so the documented path stays executable.
- **[P1 · GAP · M]** Three sources disagree about what a submission must contain — for a board with zero submissions
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/cli.py:361-365 (`compactbench submit`) instructs "3. Put your validated `results.jsonl` at `submissions/<handle>/<name>/results.jsonl`". docs/submitting.md:16-24 gives the canonical directory layout as method.py / config.yaml / requirements.txt / README.md — no results.jsonl. submissions/README.md:11-18 lists four steps with no results file. submissions/_template/ contains no results.jsonl slot. scripts/validate_submissions.py only scans for leftover `FILL_ME` strings and never checks for required files at all. There are zero submissions in the repo (`submissions/` holds only `_template/` and README.md), so nothing has ever exercised the contract.
  - *Why:* The project's entire value proposition depends on landing a first submission, and the submission contract is ambiguous at the exact moment someone tries. A submitter who follows docs/submitting.md gets told by the CLI they missed a step; one who follows the CLI adds a file the docs don't mention and CI doesn't check.
  - *Fix:* Decide whether a local results.jsonl is required (it is useful provenance but the hidden-set run is what scores). Then make all four agree: update docs/submitting.md:16-24 and submissions/README.md:11-18, add the file (or a `results.jsonl.example`) to submissions/_template/, and extend scripts/validate_submissions.py to assert the required file set so a malformed submission fails with a clear message instead of at runtime. Then dogfood it: land one maintainer submission end-to-end so the flow is proven and the board is non-empty.
- **[P1 · GAP · S]** The published docs site has no route to CONTRIBUTING or CHANGELOG
  - *Evidence:* mkdocs.yml:52-66 nav lists Home, Getting started, Writing a compactor, Integrations, Submitting, Leaderboard, Methodology, Elite program, FAQ, Governance, Architecture — no Contributing, no Changelog. Grepping docs/*.md and docs/*/*.md for `CONTRIBUTING` returns only two incidental prose mentions inside architecture/workorders.md:33 and :272. docs/index.md:33-35 ("## Source") links only the bare GitHub repo. So a visitor who lands on compactbench.github.io — the URL in every badge (README.md:9), every launch draft, and pyproject.toml:79-81 — cannot find how to contribute or what changed.
  - *Why:* The docs site is the project's front door for anyone who didn't arrive via GitHub. Contribution intent is highest right after someone reads the methodology and thinks "I could beat this" — and at that moment there is nowhere to go. It also means CHANGELOG staleness is invisible to site readers.
  - *Fix:* Add `Contributing: contributing.md` and `Changelog: changelog.md` to the mkdocs.yml nav, sourced from the root files via the already-declared `mkdocs-include-markdown-plugin` (pyproject.toml:72 declares it and mkdocs.yml has no `plugins:` block, so today it is an unused dependency), or via symlinks. Also add "Integrations" to the docs/index.md:23-31 Quick links, which currently omits it even though it is in the nav.
- **[P2 · STALE · S]** docs/architecture/decisions.md contradicts the code, the workflows, and itself
  - *Evidence:* decisions.md is titled "Technical Decisions (v1, locked)" and its revision history (line 270-272) has exactly one entry, 2026-04-16, never updated. Since then: D7 (line 181) says Anthropic/OpenAI integration awaits "a funding path" — both shipped in #19. E2 (line ~196) says the evaluate workflow "Clones `compactbench/compactbench-hidden` using a deploy key stored as a repo secret" — commit e525799 (#12) switched that to a fine-grained PAT. Part H:266 says "Every item in Parts A–F runs on a forever-free tier or is self-hosted on the Oracle free VM", directly contradicting E1 five sections earlier, which explains at length why the Oracle self-hosted runner was dropped. docs/architecture/workorders.md:207-213 likewise still specifies a `self-hosted` runner and an Astro `site/` deployed to compactbench.dev, neither of which is what shipped.
  - *Why:* These two files are the mkdocs nav's entire "Architecture" section — the only thing a serious contributor can read to understand design intent. A doc that is internally contradictory and marked "locked" while being wrong teaches contributors not to trust the docs, which multiplies every other staleness problem here.
  - *Fix:* Add revision-history rows to decisions.md:270-272 marking D7, E2, and Part H superseded, with the PR numbers; fix the Oracle sentence at Part H:266. Mark workorders.md WO-002 … WO-010 as ✅ Complete the way WO-001 is at line 19-25 (only WO-001 carries a status, so the doc reads as if 90% of the project is unbuilt), and correct WO-002's "Three sample templates" (line 59) and WO-009's "3 families" (line 233).
- **[P2 · GAP · M]** No architecture overview for contributors — only pre-launch planning artifacts
  - *Evidence:* The mkdocs.yml:63-66 Architecture section contains exactly two pages, both pre-launch planning documents: decisions.md ("v1, locked", written 2026-04-16 before most code existed) and workorders.md ("Implementation plan from post-scaffold to public PyPI launch"). Neither describes the shipped code layout. There is no page explaining that `dsl/` parses templates, `engine/` generates cases, `compactors/` holds methods, `runner/` orchestrates cycles (run.py → cycle.py → evaluation.py), `scoring/` scores, `leaderboard/` ranks — or how `--method` resolution works (runner/_resolver.py), which is the single most useful thing for a submitter to understand.
  - *Why:* Outside contributors are showing up now (#37 is a well-made provider PR). The faster they can orient in the code, the more of them convert. Asking someone to reverse-engineer package structure from a workorder plan written before the code existed is friction the project cannot afford at four contributors' worth of interest.
  - *Fix:* Add `docs/architecture/overview.md` to the nav ahead of decisions.md: one paragraph per top-level package under src/compactbench/, the run-time call path (cli.run → runner/run.py:run_experiment → runner/cycle.py:execute_cycle → scoring), the two `--method` spec forms with a pointer to runner/_resolver.py:14-57, and where to add a provider vs a compactor vs a template family. The mermaid flow already in docs/methodology.md:7-19 is a good model to follow.
- **[P2 · GAP · M]** No citation file, no related-work page — invisible to the research audience the framing targets
  - *Evidence:* No CITATION.cff in the repo root. docs/faq.md:65-72 says "We don't have a paper yet. For now, cite the repo:" followed by an unstructured two-line blob — no BibTeX, no version field, no authors. docs/architecture/decisions.md:250 lists "Research paper + arXiv submission" under Part G deferred. Meanwhile docs/index.md:13 explicitly positions against "SWE-bench, MMLU, HELM, Aider" and docs/faq.md:3-9 compares to MMLU/SWE-bench — but nothing anywhere compares CompactBench to the actual adjacent work on long-context and memory evaluation (LongBench, ∞Bench, RULER, LoCoMo, LongMemEval, HELMET), or explains what those measure that this doesn't and vice versa.
  - *Why:* The project's own positioning claims a research lineage, and a reader who knows that literature will immediately ask "how is this different from LongMemEval?" — unanswered, they assume the authors don't know the field. And with no BibTeX, anyone who does want to cite it in a paper or blog post has to invent a format, which suppresses exactly the citations that would drive submissions.
  - *Fix:* Add a CITATION.cff at the repo root (GitHub renders a "Cite this repository" button from it automatically) and replace docs/faq.md:65-72 with a real BibTeX `@software` entry including version and URL. Add `docs/related-work.md` to the nav with a short table: for each of LongBench / RULER / LoCoMo / LongMemEval / MemGPT-style evals, what it measures and the one sentence on why compaction-layer state fidelity under multi-cycle drift is not covered. This is also the strongest content you could point at from a relaunch post.
- **[P2 · STRATEGY · S]** Launch posts were never published and are now stale in every particular
  - *Evidence:* docs/launch/ contains hacker-news.md, r-localllama.md, ai-twitter.md, excluded from the site build by mkdocs.yml:70-72 ("unpublished marketing drafts"). Evidence they were never posted: exactly 4 issues have ever been filed on the repo, only one by an outsider and only today (#38); the leaderboard has zero entries; only two outside PRs exist. All three drafts are now stale: hacker-news.md:15-19 and ai-twitter.md:19-24 describe "three failure modes", hacker-news.md:23 says "Public practice templates (15 of them)", hacker-news.md:25 says "Works with Groq, Google AI Studio, Ollama out of the box" (no Anthropic/OpenAI, no LangChain/LlamaIndex, no Colab notebook), and all three link "Leaderboard: …/leaderboard/" — which renders "0 entries".
  - *Why:* Launching to HN or r/LocalLLaMA with an empty leaderboard is precisely what produced issue #38's complaint, at scale. If these get posted as-is now, the top comment is "where are the results?" and the project burns its one launch window. Conversely the drafts are decent and the missing prerequisite is small: published baselines.
  - *Fix:* Do not post until the P0 items land (0.2.0 released, baselines published at docs/results.md, #38 answered). Then refresh all three: 4 families / 20 templates, five providers, the Colab badge, LangChain + LlamaIndex, and lead with the baseline table rather than the leaderboard URL — "here are four baselines, beat them" is a far better hook for a young benchmark than an empty board. r-localllama.md is the strongest draft to lead with given the Ollama support and the local-model audience.
- **[P2 · IMPROVEMENT · S]** Empty `hard` suite ships in the wheel and is advertised by `suites list`
  - *Evidence:* benchmarks/public/hard/ contains only .gitkeep. `compactbench suites list` renders a row `hard | 0 | —` alongside elite_practice and starter, with no indication it is a placeholder. `compactbench run --suite hard --provider mock` fails with `no templates in suite 'hard'`. pyproject.toml:95-97 force-includes `benchmarks/public` into the wheel, so the empty directory ships to every pip user — confirmed: `compactbench/_data/benchmarks/public/hard/.gitkeep` is present in dist/compactbench-0.1.0-py3-none-any.whl. No doc mentions a `hard` suite. benchmarks/samples/ is similarly empty.
  - *Why:* Small, but it is on the very first exploratory command a new user runs. Advertising a suite that errors when used is a needless "is this thing finished?" signal at minute one.
  - *Fix:* Either populate `hard` or delete benchmarks/public/hard/ and benchmarks/samples/. If keeping the placeholder, make cli.py:suites_list skip zero-template directories, or label them "(placeholder — no templates yet)" and exclude them from the wheel.
- **[P2 · IMPROVEMENT · S]** Two empty, unreferenced docs directories — one shadowing the real governance page
  - *Evidence:* docs/runbooks/ and docs/benchmark-governance/ each contain only a .gitkeep. Neither appears in the mkdocs.yml nav, and grepping docs/, the root *.md files, and mkdocs.yml for `runbooks` or `benchmark-governance` returns zero references. Confirmed neither is emitted by the build (`mkdocs build --strict` output contains no such directories). Meanwhile the real, populated governance page lives at docs/governance/elite-versions.md.
  - *Why:* An empty `docs/benchmark-governance/` sitting one directory away from a populated `docs/governance/` invites a contributor to add a page to the wrong one, where it will silently never render. Pure noise in the repo a new contributor browses first.
  - *Fix:* Delete both directories. If runbooks are actually planned, open an issue for them instead of reserving a directory.
- **[P2 · RISK · S]** Docs CI only runs on push to main — no docs build on pull requests
  - *Evidence:* .github/workflows/docs.yml:3-6 triggers on `push: branches: [main]` and `workflow_dispatch` only. The build step (line 30) is `uv run mkdocs build --strict`, which does fail on broken internal links. So a docs PR from an outside contributor gets no build check; a `--strict` failure only surfaces after merge, and it fails the Pages deploy for the whole site. (I verified `mkdocs build --strict` currently passes at HEAD, so this is latent, not active.)
  - *Why:* Docs are the contribution type CONTRIBUTING.md:12 most explicitly invites ("PR to `docs/` directly"). Letting those PRs merge unchecked and then breaking the live site is the worst possible outcome for a first-time contributor's first PR.
  - *Fix:* Add `pull_request:` to the docs.yml trigger and split the workflow so the `build` job runs on PRs while `deploy` (lines 36-45) stays gated on `github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'`. While there: CONTRIBUTING.md:33 tells contributors to install `".[dev,providers]"`, but the `dev` *extra* (pyproject.toml:59-69) omits mkdocs — only the `[dependency-groups] dev` at line 110-122 has it — so a docs contributor following CONTRIBUTING cannot run `mkdocs serve`. Add `docs` to the documented install and add a "preview the docs" section.
- **[P2 · STALE · S]** Governance doc violates its own version policy — a family was added with no Elite version bump
  - *Evidence:* docs/governance/elite-versions.md:33-39 states the policy: "Adding a new family → minor — `v1` → `v2`", and lines 60-69 give a six-step procedure ending "4. Bump the Elite minor version. 5. Update this document … 6. Create a new leaderboard version." PR #27 (b7256d2, 2026-04-19) added the `reference_resolution` family. The doc's active-version table (lines 13-19) still lists three families, and the changelog (lines 86-92) still ends at "v1.0 — 2026-04-17 (active)". docs/elite-program.md:48 likewise still reads "**Current version**: Elite v1.0 (released 2026-04-17)".
  - *Why:* This project's core differentiator is governance discipline — index.md:13 and elite-program.md:3-9 sell "versioned, ranked, hardened against overfitting, maintained as a program." The first time the policy was tested, it was ignored and the log was not updated. A prospective submitter who checks whether the governance is real finds it isn't, which undercuts the reason to trust a hidden-set ranking at all. It's also a live correctness question: are the hidden ranked templates for reference_resolution in place, and does the empty leaderboard need segmenting by Elite version?
  - *Fix:* Either bump to Elite v1.1/v2 and add the changelog entry with the family, seed-group decision, and hidden-set status, or add an explicit note that reference_resolution is public-practice-only and not yet ranked. Update the active-version table at elite-versions.md:13-19 and the version line at elite-program.md:48 either way. Since there are zero leaderboard entries, this is free to fix now and expensive to fix after the first submission lands.
- **[P2 · RISK · S]** Code of Conduct routes reports to an email at a domain the project does not serve
  - *Evidence:* CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md:50 directs reports to `conduct@compactbench.dev`. There is no CNAME file anywhere in the repo; mkdocs.yml:3 sets `site_url: https://compactbench.github.io/compactbench` and every badge and link uses github.io. `compactbench.dev` appears only as a plan: decisions.md:151 ("registered via a standard registrar (Porkbun or Namecheap — post-scaffold decision)"), decisions.md:261 ("Domain registration ~$12/year"), and README.md:99 lists "custom domain" as unfinished post-launch work. SECURITY.md correctly uses GitHub private advisories instead, so this is isolated to the CoC.
  - *Why:* CONTRIBUTING.md:98 requires participants to agree to the CoC, and the CoC's only enforcement channel is an address that likely bounces. For a project actively soliciting outside contributors, an unreachable conduct channel is a real safety gap, not a formality — and the first person to need it is the one who discovers it doesn't work.
  - *Fix:* Change CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md:50 to a channel that demonstrably works today — GitHub private vulnerability reporting is already wired up, or name the maintainers' actual contact. Revisit once compactbench.dev is registered and a CNAME lands.

### The empty leaderboard

*Issue #38, the funnel, and baselines — 18 findings*

The leaderboard is empty because the submission funnel is not operable end-to-end and, if it were, it would reject every honest submission. I walked it as a hostile outsider: the `evaluate` label that gates the whole pipeline does not exist in the repo; `evaluate-submission.yml` has fired 36 times and every single run was `skipped`/`cancelled` (all dependabot label events) — the hidden-set clone, the ranked run, and the score comment have never executed once; and the last time `update-leaderboard.yml` actually tried to publish (run 24621654116, 2026-04-19) its "Commit updated leaderboard" step FAILED. Worse, the qualification floors are arithmetically incompatible with the shipped content: practice transcripts are a median of 159 tokens, so the `Elite-Mid` floor the submission template defaults to demands a ≤41-token artifact — every real compactor gets "disqualified: compression below floor". Meanwhile `scripts/rebuild_leaderboard.py` performs zero provenance checking: I fabricated a `results.jsonl` by hand (0 drift cycles, public `elite_practice` suite, "easy" difficulty, invented seed group) and it published as rank 1 with `elite_score` 0.924. The good news for #38: the maintainers' own missing baseline table costs **$36.88** of API spend across 4 compactors × 4 models, computed with the repo's own `--estimate` projector — this is a weekend of work, not a funding problem.

- **[P0 · BUG · L]** Compression tier floors are unreachable against the shipped content — every honest submission is auto-disqualified
  - *Evidence:* src/compactbench/leaderboard/ranking.py:14-18 sets TIER_FLOORS Elite-Light 2.0x / Elite-Mid 4.0x / Elite-Aggressive 8.0x, and qualification.py:39-43 rejects any run below its declared tier's floor. But the shipped transcripts are tiny. Measured with the repo's own tokenizer over benchmarks/public/elite_practice (20 templates, slot 0): mean 164 tok at medium difficulty, 262 at hard, 455 at elite. That means Elite-Mid demands an artifact <=41 tokens (medium) and Elite-Aggressive <=20 tokens. src/compactbench/runner/estimate.py:36 states the observed median compact-call output across the four built-in baselines is 500 tokens — a ratio of 0.33x, i.e. the 'compaction' is 3x LARGER than the input. The cause is engine/distractors.py:16-45: distractor turns are single one-line sentences (~10-15 tok), so even 32 of them (the `elite` policy in e.g. benchmarks/public/elite_practice/buried_constraint_v1_1.yaml:9-13) only reach ~455 tokens. Closing the loop: submissions/_template/config.yaml:12 defaults `compression_tier: Elite-Mid`, and .github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml:136-144 never passes --difficulty so ranked runs execute at medium.
  - *Why:* This alone guarantees an empty leaderboard forever. A submitter who does everything right, copies the scaffold, and gets a maintainer to run the hidden set receives 'compression 1.30x is below the Elite-Mid floor of 4.0x' and leaves. It also undermines the product claim: a compaction benchmark whose transcripts are 160 tokens is not measuring compaction — real compaction fires at 50k-200k tokens of history.
  - *Fix:* Two changes, both needed. (1) Scale the content: make distractor turns multi-sentence and add a `filler_tokens` knob to the difficulty policy so medium ~4k tok and elite ~20k+ tok transcripts, then bump the suite version. Cost impact is negligible — the estimator shows elite vs medium difficulty moves an Anthropic Haiku run from $5.47 to $5.94 (+9%) because output tokens dominate. (2) Until (1) lands, re-derive TIER_FLOORS from real measured baselines (see the baseline-table finding) instead of round numbers, and change submissions/_template/config.yaml:12 to `Elite-Light` so the scaffold is not a guaranteed rejection. Add a unit test asserting each built-in compactor clears its declared tier on the practice suite.
- **[P0 · BUG · M]** rebuild_leaderboard.py ranks any hand-written results.jsonl — I fabricated a rank-1 entry
  - *Evidence:* scripts/rebuild_leaderboard.py:54-120 reads submissions/*/*/results.jsonl, calls to_run_result() (runner/persistence.py:154), qualifies, and publishes. There is no signature, no run-id allowlist, no check that suite_key is the hidden ranked suite, and no marker that the file came from the maintainer runner. I wrote a results.jsonl by hand with `suite_key=elite_practice` (the PUBLIC suite), `difficulty=easy`, `seed_group='my-own-lucky-seeds'`, `drift_cycles=0`, 3 cases per template, all scores 1.0 — plus a matching config.yaml — and ran the real script: `wrote docs/data/leaderboard.json with 1 entries`, rank 1, elite_score 0.92375, tier Elite-Aggressive. The public page (docs/leaderboard.md:76-87) would render it identically to a genuine ranked row; the only tell is `benchmark_version: elite_practice@1.0.0` in a column most readers cannot interpret. The 'provenance' fix in PR #26 (evaluate-submission.yml:176-211) only overwrites results.jsonl for same-repo PRs — evaluate-submission.yml:177 skips it for forks, which is every outside submission.
  - *Why:* The first fraudulent or merely careless submission poisons the board, and the board's whole value proposition is 'hidden ranked set keeps everyone honest' (docs/index.md:7). Publishing numbers you cannot vouch for is worse than publishing none.
  - *Fix:* Add an unforgeable provenance step: have the evaluate workflow write a sibling `results.provenance.json` containing the workflow run id, run attempt, head SHA, hidden-repo commit SHA, and an HMAC over the results.jsonl SHA-256 using a repo secret; make rebuild_leaderboard.py refuse any submission whose results lack a valid provenance record. Separately (cheap and immediate), add hard gates in rebuild_leaderboard.py: reject unless `suite_key` is in an explicit RANKED_SUITES allowlist, `seed_group` matches the version's pinned group, `difficulty` matches the ranked profile, and `drift_cycles >= 2`. Add tests — scripts/rebuild_leaderboard.py currently has zero test coverage (nothing under tests/ references it).
- **[P0 · BUG · S]** drift_cycles is submitter-controlled and `drift_cycles: 0` hands over 30% of elite_score for free
  - *Evidence:* scoring/drift.py:26-28 returns drift_resistance 1.0 when there are fewer than 2 cycle scores. leaderboard/ranking.py:47 weights drift_resistance at 0.30. qualification.py:54-60 checks completion against `expected_drift_cycles`, which rebuild_leaderboard.py:81-87 reads from the SUBMITTER's own config.yaml (`runtime.drift_cycles`, defaulting to 2), and evaluate-submission.yml:125 likewise passes `--drift-cycles` straight from the submitter's config. So declaring `drift_cycles: 0` both skips the drift test and passes the completeness check. My fabricated entry did exactly this and scored drift_resistance 1.0.
  - *Why:* Multi-cycle drift is the headline differentiator on the README (line 18). A leaderboard whose top entry opted out of the drift test — legally, via a config field — is not just wrong, it is wrong in precisely the dimension the project claims to own. Anyone who reads the code before submitting will notice this and conclude the ranking is unserious.
  - *Fix:* Ranked runs must use a fixed, maintainer-controlled profile. In evaluate-submission.yml, stop reading drift_cycles/case_count/seed_group/difficulty from the submitter's config (lines 122-127) and hard-code the ranked profile; leave only `provider`, `model`, `compression_tier`, and `class_path` submitter-settable. In qualification.py, replace the `expected_drift_cycles` parameter with a module constant `RANKED_DRIFT_CYCLES = 2` and reject runs whose run_start declares fewer. Also drop `published_at=run_result.completed_at` (rebuild_leaderboard.py:118) — it is submitter-controlled and is the final tie-breaker in ranking.py:70, so a backdated timestamp wins every tie; use the merge commit date instead.
- **[P0 · STALE · M]** The submission pipeline has never executed once and its gating label does not exist
  - *Evidence:* evaluate-submission.yml:25 gates the job on `github.event.label.name == 'evaluate'`, and docs/submitting.md:54 documents a `reevaluate` label for re-runs. Neither label exists in the repository — the GitHub API returns "label 'evaluate' not found in compactbench/compactbench" and the same for 'reevaluate'. The workflow has 36 runs; every one is `skipped` or `cancelled`, all triggered by dependabot `labeled` events (e.g. run 28053620676, 2026-06-23). The 'Clone hidden ranked set', 'Run against ranked suite', 'Post score comment' steps have never run. `reevaluate` is also implemented nowhere: grep over .github/workflows finds only the `evaluate` string, so applying it does nothing and re-evaluation actually requires removing and re-adding `evaluate`. Meanwhile docs/leaderboard.md:42 tells visitors to watch "open PRs with the `evaluate` label" — a query that can only ever be empty.
  - *Why:* This is the literal answer to issue #38. Everything downstream — HIDDEN_REPO_TOKEN validity, the hidden repo's directory layout (`/tmp/hidden/ranked/elite_v1`), the GROQ/GOOGLE secrets, the score-comment step — is unverified. If alexander-potemkin or jaaabir submitted tomorrow, the maintainer would be debugging CI in public on someone else's PR.
  - *Fix:* Create the `evaluate` and `reevaluate` labels; make evaluate-submission.yml accept either (`contains(fromJSON('["evaluate","reevaluate"]'), github.event.label.name)`). Then dogfood the whole flow before advertising it: open a real PR at submissions/UsernameLoad/hybrid-ledger-baseline/ wrapping the built-in hybrid-ledger, label it, and drive it to a merged, ranked leaderboard row. That single exercise validates the token, the hidden repo layout, the fork-vs-same-repo commit path, and the publish step — and it also gives the board its first entry.
- **[P0 · BUG · S]** The leaderboard publish step failed the one time it actually tried to push, and `[skip ci]` blocks the Pages redeploy anyway
  - *Evidence:* update-leaderboard.yml has exactly 2 runs. Run 24621654116 (2026-04-19, commit 1d31973) completed with conclusion=failure; the job's step list shows 'Rebuild leaderboard.json' succeeded and step 7 'Commit updated leaderboard' failed (logs are 410 Gone now). scripts/rebuild_leaderboard.py:126-144 was later patched (in #25) to preserve `updated_at` on no-op rebuilds, with a comment stating the push "branch protection rightly rejects" — but that only suppresses empty diffs. A real submission produces a real diff, and the same `git push` at update-leaderboard.yml:53 hits the same protection. Separately, the commit message at line 52 is `chore(leaderboard): rebuild from merged submissions [skip ci]`, while the workflow header at lines 3-5 claims "The commit we push triggers the docs workflow which redeploys GitHub Pages." `[skip ci]` suppresses push-triggered workflow runs, and docs.yml:3-5 triggers on push to main — so even on success the site never redeploys with the new JSON.
  - *Why:* Both halves of the publish path are broken, and neither has ever succeeded with real content. A submission could pass evaluation, be merged, and still never appear on the public page — the exact outcome that makes a leaderboard look dead.
  - *Fix:* Remove `[skip ci]` from update-leaderboard.yml:52 (use `paths-ignore` on docs.yml if you need loop protection instead). For the push: either add a branch-protection bypass for github-actions[bot], or — better — stop committing generated state to main entirely. Move the `python scripts/rebuild_leaderboard.py` call into docs.yml's build job right before `mkdocs build`, so docs/data/leaderboard.json is derived at deploy time from the committed submissions and never needs a bot push. Add `workflow_dispatch:` so it can be re-run by hand.
- **[P0 · RISK · L]** Ranked evaluation runs untrusted submitter code in the same job as the hidden-set PAT — the leak vector SECURITY.md names as in-scope
  - *Evidence:* evaluate-submission.yml:8 uses `pull_request_target` (trusted context, repo secrets) and line 34 checks out the submitter's `head.sha`; line 84 pip-installs the submitter's requirements.txt; lines 136-144 execute the submitter's method.py. In the same job, lines 104-106 `git clone https://x-access-token:${HIDDEN_REPO_TOKEN}@github.com/compactbench/compactbench-hidden.git /tmp/hidden` — which both places all 60 hidden templates on disk and writes the PAT into /tmp/hidden/.git/config — and lines 133-134 put COMPACTBENCH_GROQ_API_KEY and COMPACTBENCH_GOOGLE_AI_STUDIO_API_KEY directly in the run step's env. Submitter code trivially reads all three. decisions.md §E3 calls maintainer code review "the security gate", but SECURITY.md:24 lists "Any leak vector that lets a submitter view hidden ranked templates" as an in-scope vulnerability, and SECURITY.md:29 claims "the runner enforces rate and size limits" — there is no timeout-minutes, no size cap, and no rate cap anywhere in the workflow. Two smaller injections in the same file: line 234 interpolates `${{ steps.summary.outputs.body }}` (which contains the submitter-chosen method name, via `compactor_cls.name`) into a JS template literal in actions/github-script; and lines 114-128 write submitter-controlled config.yaml values into $GITHUB_ENV with no newline sanitisation. Note the org has an unused private repo `compactbench/hardened-action` created 2026-04-20, suggesting this was already recognised and then dropped.
  - *Why:* A one-person project cannot code-review its way to safety against an adversary whose whole incentive is to see the hidden set. The first popular submission is also the first plausible attack, and the hidden set is the asset that makes the ranking defensible at all. Publishing 'evaluated on hidden Elite cases' after a silent leak is the reputational end of the project.
  - *Fix:* Split into two jobs with no shared secret surface. Job A (trusted, no submitter code): clone the hidden set, generate cases, and upload the generated case bundle as an internal artifact — do not use `git clone` with the token in the URL, use `actions/checkout` with the token so credentials are not persisted to disk. Job B: run the submitter's compactor in a container with `--network=none` against the case bundle, with the provider calls proxied by the trusted job, and with `timeout-minutes` set. If that is too much for v1, the honest interim is to stop advertising maintainer-run hidden evaluation and ship the self-serve public-suite board described below.
- **[P0 · GAP · M]** Nobody has ever published baseline numbers, and the whole missing table costs $36.88
  - *Evidence:* docs/data/leaderboard.json is `{"entries": [], "updated_at": "2026-04-17T00:00:00+00:00"}` — the public page (docs/leaderboard.md:33) renders "Updated 2026-04-17 00:00:00 UTC · 0 entries" to every visitor, four months stale. No results.jsonl exists anywhere in the repo. Nothing in the codebase records what the four built-in compactors actually score. I priced the missing table with the repo's own projector (`compactbench run --estimate`, runner/costs.py catalogue dated 2026-04-18): one run of elite_practice at --case-count 20 --drift-cycles 2 --difficulty elite = 400 cases, 4,320 API calls, 1.30M input + 1.22M output tokens, costing $1.73 (groq/llama-3.3-70b-versatile), $0.62 (google-ai-studio/gemini-2.0-flash), $0.93 (openai/gpt-4o-mini), $5.94 (anthropic/claude-3-5-haiku-latest). Four built-in compactors x those four models = $36.88 total. Wall clock: 4,320 calls at ~11 sequential calls per case and --concurrency 4 is ~100 waves, roughly 25-30 min per (method, model) — about 7-8 hours for all 16 runs serial, ~2 hours at --concurrency 16. Note ollama/llama3.2 is in the catalogue at $0.00, so a local-only variant is free.
  - *Why:* This is exactly what issue #38 asks: "not a single run result — what to compare with?" A benchmark with no numbers is a library, not a benchmark. Baselines also do work no submission can do: they calibrate the floors, they prove the pipeline runs, and they give a would-be submitter a target to beat. At $37 the absence is not a resource problem, it is a decision that was never made.
  - *Fix:* Ship a `docs/baselines.md` page plus a machine-readable `docs/data/baselines.json`, generated by a new `scripts/run_baselines.py` that loops the four built-ins over a pinned model list and writes results.jsonl per (method, model) into `benchmarks/results/` (committed, so anyone can re-score with `compactbench score`). Pin and publish the exact profile: suite `elite_practice@<version>`, `--case-count 20 --drift-cycles 2 --difficulty elite --seed-group elite_v1_q1`, scorer 1.0.0, plus the date, per-run token usage (the runner already records TokenUsage), and measured dollar cost. Start with the two cheapest models (gemini-2.0-flash + gpt-4o-mini = $6.20 for all four methods) if you want a same-day result. Publish per-family breakdowns — `compactbench score` already computes item_type_breakdown (cli.py:288-306) — because "naive-summary collapses on decision_override" is the finding that makes people care.
- **[P1 · RISK · M]** Ranked evaluation is economically impossible on the free tiers the design pins
  - *Evidence:* decisions.md Part H budgets the entire project at "~$12/year" with "Hosting, CI, docs, leaderboard, runner | $0", and §D6 pins leaderboard evaluation to "Groq free tier: llama-3.3-70b-versatile". evaluate-submission.yml:133-134 wires only GROQ_API_KEY and GOOGLE_AI_STUDIO_API_KEY. But governance/elite-versions.md:19 says the hidden set is 60 templates, and submissions/_template/config.yaml:14 defaults `case_count_per_template: 20` -> 1,200 cases per evaluation. Scaling from the measured estimate (400 cases = 1.95M tokens): 1,200 cases ~ 5.84M tokens, versus the 100,000 tokens/day Groq free cap hard-coded at runner/costs.py:55. That is 58x the daily cap; even 1 case per hidden template (60 cases) is 2.9x over. providers/_retry.py:37 classifies daily-quota 429s as terminal, and runner/run.py:174-179 cancels every task and re-raises on any exception, so the run aborts with no run_end event — and the workflow passes no `--resume` and sets no `timeout-minutes`. getting-started.md:41 already concedes this ("exceeds Groq's free-tier daily token limit. For real evaluation runs use Anthropic, OpenAI, or a paid Groq account") while §D6 still says the opposite.
  - *Why:* The first real submission would burn a maintainer afternoon on a 429 traceback, not a score. And a single transient provider 500 anywhere in 12,960 calls kills the entire run with no partial credit. This is why 'maintainer runs it against the hidden set' cannot work for a one-person project at the configured scale.
  - *Fix:* Three things. (1) Correct decisions.md Part H and §D6 with a real ranked-evaluation budget line (~$2-6 per submission at 300-1200 cases) and wire an ANTHROPIC/OPENAI secret, or fund a paid Groq account. (2) Cap the ranked profile at `--case-count 5` over 60 hidden templates (300 cases, ~$0.5-1.5/run) — statistical power comes from 60 distinct templates, not 20 seeds each. (3) Make the runner resilient: catch per-case exceptions in run.py's `_run_one`, record a failed CaseResult, and let qualification.py reject on failures instead of losing the whole run; add `--resume` and `timeout-minutes: 120` to the workflow.
- **[P1 · RISK · M]** Fork PRs — i.e. every outside submission — need a manual maintainer file-copy, or the board silently ranks the submitter's own file
  - *Evidence:* evaluate-submission.yml:176-177 commits the canonical hidden-run results back to the PR branch only `if github.event.pull_request.head.repo.full_name == github.repository`. Lines 213-224 handle the fork case with a `core.warning(...)` telling the maintainer to "download the `results` artifact from this run and commit it to the submission directory before merging." That warning appears only in the Actions log, not as a PR comment or a merge-blocking check. Meanwhile cli.py:376-378 (`compactbench submit`) instructs the submitter to "Put your validated results.jsonl at submissions/<handle>/<name>/results.jsonl" — so the submitter's own file is already sitting there, and rebuild_leaderboard.py will rank it if the maintainer forgets.
  - *Why:* Outside contributors submit from forks essentially 100% of the time (PR #37 from jaaabir is a fork PR). The default failure mode is silent: the leaderboard publishes a self-reported number while the site says it was hidden-set evaluated. That is the single most damaging thing a benchmark can do.
  - *Fix:* Make the fork path fail closed. Have the evaluate job post the fork warning as a PR comment AND set a failing commit status/check (`Ranked results not committed`) that branch protection requires. Better: have the evaluate job open a small automated PR against the submitter's branch (or push the canonical results into a maintainer-owned `results/<handle>/<method>/` tree the submitter cannot write) so the artifact never depends on human memory. Also change cli.py:376-378 to stop telling submitters to commit results.jsonl at all — the canonical file should only ever be written by the runner.
- **[P1 · BUG · S]** Ranked runs silently ignore the documented seed group and run at medium difficulty
  - *Evidence:* governance/elite-versions.md:21-27 states the ranked leaderboard pins seed group `elite_v1_q1` so "Submissions are evaluated against the same seeds so scores are directly comparable", and lists seed-group rotation as the anti-overfitting mechanism (lines 39, 80). But evaluate-submission.yml:136-144 passes only --method/--benchmarks-dir/--suite/--provider/--model/--drift-cycles/--case-count. It never passes --seed-group or --difficulty, so cli.py:68 defaults seed_group to `"default"` and cli.py:56 defaults difficulty to `"medium"`. The value is recorded verbatim into the results file (runner/run.py:110-112) but never checked by qualification.py and never projected onto the public row (leaderboard/projection.py:20-40).
  - *Why:* The published anti-overfitting story is not implemented: rotating `elite_v1_q1` would change nothing because ranked runs use `default`. And the 'hardest benchmark program we maintain' (elite-program.md:7) would be ranking everyone at the medium difficulty tier — 8 distractor turns instead of 32 — for a cost difference of 9% ($5.47 vs $5.94 on Haiku).
  - *Fix:* Add `--seed-group elite_v1_q1 --difficulty elite` to evaluate-submission.yml's run step, define the ranked profile as constants in one place (a `benchmarks/ranked-profile.yaml` read by both the workflow and rebuild_leaderboard.py), and have rebuild_leaderboard.py reject any results.jsonl whose run_start does not match the profile exactly. This is a ~10-line change that makes the governance doc true.
- **[P1 · GAP · S]** The public row hides everything a reader needs to trust the number
  - *Evidence:* leaderboard/projection.py:20-40 defines the public LeaderboardRow: rank, method name/version, handle, org, tier, benchmark_version, provider, model, scorer_version, the five metrics, published_at. It omits difficulty, seed_group, drift_cycles, case_count_per_template, total case count, token usage (which the runner does record — contracts/result.py:124), measured cost, and any link to the evaluation run. rank_rows (projection.py:91-97) segments on (benchmark_version, provider, model, scorer_version) only — so a 3-case, 0-drift-cycle, easy-difficulty run competes head-to-head in the same segment as a 1,200-case, 2-cycle, elite-difficulty run, as my fabricated entry demonstrated.
  - *Why:* An outsider evaluating whether to trust the board cannot answer "how many cases was this?" or "can I reproduce it?" from the page. Reproducibility is claimed as a core property (docs/index.md:17) but nothing published is sufficient to reproduce anything.
  - *Fix:* Extend LeaderboardRow with `difficulty`, `seed_group`, `drift_cycles`, `case_count`, `total_cases`, `total_api_calls`, `prompt_tokens`/`completion_tokens`, `evaluation_run_url`, and `results_path` (the in-repo path to the committed results.jsonl), bump SCHEMA_VERSION in rebuild_leaderboard.py:38, and render the reproduction command per row in docs/leaderboard.md as an expandable cell. Add drift_cycles and case_count to the segment key so incomparable runs cannot share a rank.
- **[P1 · GAP · S]** A submitter cannot check whether their run would qualify — there is no `compactbench qualify`
  - *Evidence:* The CLI exposes run/generate/score/submit/providers/suites (cli.py:48, 182, 246, 346, 396, 423). `compactbench score` (cli.py:246-343) prints overall_score, drift_resistance, constraint_retention, contradiction_rate, compression_ratio and a per-item-type breakdown — but never elite_score and never qualification status. The leaderboard package is fully importable and already returns actionable rejection strings (qualification.py:36-74 produces e.g. "compression 1.30x is below the Elite-Mid floor of 4.0x"), but nothing surfaces it to a user. So the only way to learn you would be rejected is to open a PR and get a maintainer to spend hidden-set budget on you.
  - *Why:* This is the single cheapest funnel fix in the repo. Combined with the unreachable compression floors, it means every prospective submitter today would burn a maintainer's evaluation run to receive a rejection they could have seen locally in one second.
  - *Fix:* Add `compactbench qualify --results results.jsonl --tier Elite-Mid [--drift-cycles 2]` that calls leaderboard.qualify + leaderboard.elite_score and prints PASS/FAIL, the elite_score with its four weighted components broken out, and each rejection reason. Add elite_score to the `score` command's Aggregate table too. Then reference it in docs/submitting.md as a required pre-PR step and in submissions/_template/method.py's docstring. Roughly 40 lines plus tests.
- **[P1 · STRATEGY · L]** Recommended design: a two-tier board — self-serve Community tier now, maintainer-run Ranked tier when the pipeline is proven
  - *Evidence:* Everything above shows that 'maintainer runs it against the hidden set' is not viable for this project today: the gating label doesn't exist, the pipeline has never run (36 skipped runs), the publish step failed its one real attempt, the secret model leaks the hidden set to submitter code by design, and the free-tier budget in decisions.md Part H is 58x short. Yet the public suite is fully self-service and cheap: benchmarks/public/elite_practice ships 20 templates across 4 families, case generation is deterministic from (template@version, seed_group, slot) (runner/run.py:129-131), results.jsonl already carries every version stamp needed for provenance (persistence.py:29-45), and a full 400-case run costs $0.62-$5.94 depending on model — or $0 on Ollama.
  - *Why:* The project has outside interest RIGHT NOW (issue #38 today, PR #37 open 18 days) and a leaderboard that cannot accept an entry. A self-reported public tier converts that interest into rows this month; a hidden ranked tier that requires a maintainer in the loop converts it into nothing.
  - *Fix:* Ship two boards on one page. **Community (self-reported, public suite)**: submitter runs the pinned public profile locally, PRs `submissions/<handle>/<method>/{method.py, config.yaml, results.jsonl}`; CI (a plain `pull_request` job, no secrets) re-runs `compactbench score` on the committed file to verify the aggregates match the events, runs the qualification check, verifies the run_start profile matches the pinned ranked profile, and — the key trick — re-executes 3 randomly chosen cases with the mock provider to confirm the case_ids and seeds are genuinely derivable from the declared (suite, seed_group, slot). Rows are labelled 'self-reported' with a link to the exact reproduction command. **Ranked (hidden set, maintainer-run)**: unchanged in intent, but marked 'opening soon' until the pipeline is dogfooded, and gated on the sandboxing fix. Seed the Community board with the four maintainer baselines so it is never empty. This design gives you rows without a maintainer bottleneck, keeps the honest label on self-reported numbers, and preserves the hidden set's value for when it can be operated safely.
- **[P1 · STRATEGY · S]** Draft reply to issue #38
  - *Evidence:* Issue #38 ("not a single bench run?", alexander-potemkin, filed 2026-08-23) is unanswered, unlabelled, and has zero comments. It is the only inbound signal the project has received in four months and it names the exact problem this review confirms.
  - *Why:* The reply is the project's only current chance to convert an interested outsider into a contributor. A vague "good point, we'll get to it" loses him; a dated, costed, specific plan with a first deliverable is what keeps someone like this around — and he may well run the baselines himself.
  - *Fix:* Post something close to this, then label the issue `leaderboard` + `help wanted` and pin it:

---
You're right, and thanks for asking directly — this is the most important issue in the repo right now.

The honest answer: the board is empty because we built the submission pipeline and then never ran it. Concretely, (a) the `evaluate` label that gates `.github/workflows/evaluate-submission.yml` was never created, so the hidden-set evaluation has literally never executed; (b) the last real run of `update-leaderboard.yml` failed on its commit step back in April; and (c) we never published our own baselines for the four built-in compactors, which is the thing you'd actually want to compare against. No submissions, no numbers, no excuse.

What I'm doing about it, in order:

1. **Baselines first (this week).** All four built-ins (`naive-summary`, `structured-state`, `hierarchical-summary`, `hybrid-ledger`) on the public `elite_practice` suite, pinned profile: 20 templates x 20 cases x 2 drift cycles at elite difficulty. Using the repo's own `compactbench run --estimate`, that's 400 cases / 4,320 API calls / ~2.5M tokens per (method, model), which prices at $0.62 on gemini-2.0-flash, $0.93 on gpt-4o-mini, $1.73 on groq/llama-3.3-70b, $5.94 on claude-3-5-haiku. The whole 4x4 matrix is $36.88. I'll commit every `results.jsonl` to the repo so you can re-score them yourself with `compactbench score` and reproduce them with the printed command — the cases are deterministic from (template@version, seed_group, slot).
2. **A self-serve Community tier.** You shouldn't need me in the loop to get a number on the board. Public-suite results, submitted by PR, verified in CI (aggregates re-derived from the event log, case seeds re-derived from the declared profile), and labelled 'self-reported' so it's clear what they are. Runs on Ollama for $0 if you don't want to spend anything.
3. **Two things I found while auditing this that you should know about before you invest time:** the compression tier floors (2x/4x/8x) are currently unreachable because the shipped practice transcripts are only ~160-460 tokens — I'm scaling the transcripts up, which is also what makes this a realistic compaction benchmark. And `compactbench score` doesn't yet print `elite_score` or qualification status, so you can't tell locally whether a run would rank; a `compactbench qualify` command is going in.

The hidden ranked tier stays, but it stays marked 'opening soon' until I've driven a real submission through it end to end rather than claiming it works.

If you're interested in compaction methods specifically, the fastest way to be useful is to run any of the four built-ins on a model I'm not covering and open a PR with the results — I'll take that as the first Community row. And if you want to see the shape of what's coming, PR #37 (base_url on the OpenAI provider) unlocks vLLM/llama.cpp/LM Studio/OpenRouter, which makes all of this free to run locally; I'm merging it.
---
- **[P1 · RISK · M]** Qualification floors and rejection reasons were never calibrated against data
  - *Evidence:* MAX_CONTRADICTION_RATE = 0.10 and MIN_FAMILY_MEAN_SCORE = 0.40 (qualification.py:13-14) and TIER_FLOORS 2/4/8x (ranking.py:14-18) are round numbers with no empirical backing anywhere in the repo. The compression_bonus (ranking.py:21-30) is 0 at the floor and saturates at 1.0 at 2x the floor — for Elite-Aggressive that's 16x, which the tiny transcripts make unreachable, so in practice the 0.10 compression term is a constant 0 for everyone and contributes nothing to ranking. The family guard only applies when a run covers more than one family (qualification.py:66), and the hidden set covers 3 families (governance/elite-versions.md:13-19) while the public suite now covers 4 — `reference_resolution_v1` landed 2026-04-19 in commit b7256d2, two days after the private compactbench-hidden repo's last update (2026-04-17), so users develop against 4 families and are ranked on 3.
  - *Why:* Floors that reject 100% of honest submissions and a bonus term that is identically zero are not a ranking system; they are a rejection system nobody has ever tested. And a practice/ranked family mismatch means a submitter can tune against a family that is never scored.
  - *Fix:* Do not tune the floors by intuition — derive them. Once the baselines exist, set each tier floor from the measured distribution (e.g. Elite-Light at the 25th percentile of built-in compression, Mid at the median, Aggressive at the 90th), and re-derive MIN_FAMILY_MEAN_SCORE from the weakest baseline's per-family mean so `naive-summary` sits just below it by construction. Rescale compression_bonus so it saturates at a ratio real methods reach. Publish the derivation in methodology.md and treat future changes as a scorer-version bump (as decisions.md §B4 already requires). Separately, either add `reference_resolution` ranked variations to compactbench-hidden or mark it explicitly 'practice-only, not ranked' in elite-program.md and governance/elite-versions.md.
- **[P2 · STALE · S]** The funnel's own docs point newcomers at closed issues and a command that errors
  - *Evidence:* CONTRIBUTING.md:15-23 advertises "Three planned workorders ... open as GitHub issues and labeled `help wanted`" — issues #5, #6, #7 — all three are CLOSED (verified via API; closed 2026-04-17). docs/leaderboard.md:106-110 carries a "Tracking" section linking the same three closed issues, so the leaderboard page's last section is dead links. faq.md:77 tells people "Method submission questions: comment on #5" (closed). README.md:52 and getting-started.md:102 both document `compactbench generate --template buried_constraint_v1 --seed 42`, which exits 1 with "template 'buried_constraint_v1' not found" (real keys are `buried_constraint_v1_1` ... `_5`). getting-started.md:41 says "15 templates x default 5 cases" — the suite has had 20 templates since #27. docs/submitting.md:10 says "The self-hosted runner executes your method" while decisions.md §E1 explicitly dropped self-hosted runners.
  - *Why:* These are the exact pages a person reads in the ten minutes between finding the repo and deciding whether it is alive. Dead tracking links on the leaderboard page and a second-command-fails experience read as abandonment — which, four months post-commit with an empty board, is the conclusion they were already leaning toward.
  - *Fix:* Delete the Tracking section from docs/leaderboard.md:106-110 and replace it with a link to a new pinned 'Leaderboard status' issue. Rewrite CONTRIBUTING.md:15-23 around what actually needs doing now (baselines, a Community-tier submission, new template families). Fix the `generate` examples to `buried_constraint_v1_1`, fix the 15-template count, fix faq.md:77, and correct 'self-hosted runner' in submitting.md:10.
- **[P2 · GAP · S]** Submission PRs get no automated feedback: the placeholder validator never runs and repo-wide ruff will fail their code
  - *Evidence:* scripts/validate_submissions.py (the FILL_ME checker) is invoked only inside evaluate-submission.yml:49 — i.e. after a maintainer applies a label that does not exist. ci.yml has no submissions job, so a PR adding submissions/<handle>/<method>/ receives zero submission-specific feedback. Worse, ci.yml:29-31 runs `ruff check .` and `ruff format --check .` over the whole repo, and pyproject.toml:124-145 sets no `exclude` for submissions/ with a fairly opinionated rule set (E,F,W,I,B,UP,N,SIM,RUF,PT). Pyright is scoped to src+tests (pyproject.toml:174), so that one is fine. Neither validate_submissions.py nor rebuild_leaderboard.py has any test coverage.
  - *Why:* A first-time contributor's very first experience is a red X on CI for import ordering in their own method file, with no guidance in docs/submitting.md about running ruff on submissions. Meanwhile the checks that would actually help them (placeholders resolved, config schema valid, tier declared, results profile correct) don't run at all.
  - *Fix:* Add a `submissions` job to ci.yml that runs on `pull_request` with no secrets: `scripts/validate_submissions.py`, a new config.yaml schema validation (required keys, valid tier, class_path resolves and subclasses Compactor via runner._resolver.resolve_compactor_class), and — once the Community tier exists — the results verification. Add `exclude = ["submissions"]` to [tool.ruff] (or at minimum add a `ruff format submissions/<your-dir>` line to docs/submitting.md and the PR template). Add unit tests for both scripts, including one that asserts a fabricated results.jsonl on a non-ranked suite is rejected.
- **[P2 · IMPROVEMENT · S]** Merge PR #37 — it is the cheapest path to a populated board
  - *Evidence:* PR #37 (jaaabir, open since 2026-08-05, 18 days unreviewed, mergeable_state=blocked) adds `base_url` + COMPACTBENCH_OPENAI_BASE_URL to the OpenAI provider, enabling vLLM/llama.cpp/LM Studio/Together/Fireworks/OpenRouter. Today runner/costs.py:29-49 catalogues only 5 hosted providers plus ollama, and cli.py:411-416 lists only mock/groq/google-ai-studio/ollama in `providers list`. The Groq free-tier cap (costs.py:55) is 100,000 tokens/day against a 1.95M-token practice run.
  - *Why:* The binding constraint on getting rows onto a board is per-run cost and API access. An OpenAI-compatible base_url turns every local and every aggregator endpoint into a valid target, which makes the self-serve Community tier free for anyone with a GPU — and it converts the one outside contributor the project currently has into an engaged one, 18 days late.
  - *Fix:* Review and merge #37 this week (the dependabot backlog #31-#36 is what's making it mergeable_state=blocked — clear those first). Then follow up on the gap its author flagged: add `extra_body`/`chat_template_kwargs` passthrough on CompletionRequest so reasoning models served via vLLM don't return chain-of-thought as the eval answer, which would silently corrupt any score run against them. Add `providers list` entries and a costs.py catalogue entry for `openai-compatible` at $0.00 with a note that pricing is endpoint-specific.

### Benchmark content

*Templates, realism, gameability — 21 findings*

The content layer is the weakest part of CompactBench and the part most likely to make published numbers indefensible. Three structural problems dominate: (1) the generated `Turn.tags` field hands the compactor a labelled answer key — a 5-line, zero-LLM compactor that drops turns tagged `distractor` recovers 100% of every `contains_normalized` answer at 6.37x compression, which qualifies for two of three Elite tiers; (2) the "elite" difficulty tier produces transcripts averaging 471 tokens (max 524), so compaction never actually bites and the benchmark cannot measure the phenomenon it claims to measure at any modern context length; (3) roughly 37% of evaluation items use `forbidden_absent` against well-known anti-patterns, which a compactor that emits *nothing* passes for free — an empty artifact scores 0.340 overall, 0.612 elite_score, and reports a 101x compression ratio. On top of that, all case content is drawn from a closed 101-string lexicon, four of the ten failure modes advertised in `docs/elite-program.md` have zero template coverage, `paraphrase_depth`/`override_timing` are silent no-ops so difficulty tiers differ only in filler count, and the docs still describe three families when four ship. Before any baseline numbers are published to answer issue #38, the answer key leak, the free-points items, and the scale premise need fixing — otherwise the first leaderboard will be topped by a method that does nothing.

- **[P0 · RISK · S]** Turn.tags leak the answer key directly to the compactor — a 5-line zero-LLM method scores 100% retention
  - *Evidence:* `src/compactbench/engine/transcript.py:59` copies `tags=list(template_turn.tags)` onto every generated `Turn`, and `src/compactbench/engine/distractors.py:68` stamps `tags=["distractor"]` on every noise turn. `src/compactbench/contracts/case.py:24` makes `tags` part of the `Transcript` model, and `src/compactbench/runner/cycle.py:79` passes that exact object to `compactor.compact(working_transcript, ...)`. The tags are literally named after the trap: I measured `{'critical_constraint': 130, 'distractor': 6640, 'decision_override': 20, 'final_override': 10, 'meta_reset': 10, 'late_override': 10, 'partial_override': 10, 'confusion_risk': 30, 'indirect_phrasing': 10, 'negative_example': 10, 'sandwiched': 10}` across the public suite. I built the exploit: a compactor whose entire body is `[t.content for t in transcript.turns if "distractor" not in t.tags]` recovers 400/400 (100.0%) of all `contains_normalized` expected values verbatim at a mean compression ratio of 6.37x — with zero provider calls and zero cost. That clears the Elite-Light (2.0x) and Elite-Mid (4.0x) floors in `src/compactbench/leaderboard/ranking.py:14-18`. A one-line dedup of the repeated assistant filler pushes it past the 8.0x Elite-Aggressive floor. Nothing in `src/compactbench/runner/run.py` or `src/compactbench/leaderboard/qualification.py` strips or checks for tag use.
  - *Why:* This is the single fastest way for the first-ever leaderboard submission to be a fraud, and it is undetectable from the published row (perfect retention, perfect drift resistance, high compression, zero latency). With PR #37 bringing in local-model users and issue #38 asking for numbers, the first submissions are imminent. Publishing a leaderboard that a `if 'distractor' not in t.tags` filter tops would end the project's credibility permanently.
  - *Fix:* Strip tags at the runner boundary before handing the transcript to the method. In `src/compactbench/runner/cycle.py:79`, pass `Transcript(turns=[t.model_copy(update={'tags': []}) for t in working_transcript.turns])`. Keep the tagged copy internally for scoring/diagnostics. Add a regression test asserting that every turn a compactor receives has `tags == []`. Longer term, move tags out of `Turn` entirely into a side-channel `case.turn_annotations: dict[int, list[str]]` on `GeneratedCase` so they cannot leak by construction.
- **[P0 · STRATEGY · L]** "Elite" transcripts average 471 tokens — the benchmark's core premise does not hold at any modern context scale
  - *Evidence:* I measured `transcript_tokens()` (cl100k_base) for all 20 elite_practice templates at every difficulty: easy mean 114, medium 164, hard 270, **elite mean 470.9 (min 444, max 524)**. The elite tier is 32 distractor turns (`benchmarks/public/elite_practice/*.yaml`, `difficulty_policy.distractor_turns.elite: 32`), and each distractor is one short sentence from `src/compactbench/engine/distractors.py:16-45`. The longest case in the entire public suite is `decision_override_v1_4` at elite = 524 tokens across 41 turns. Drift cycles add only two short turns each (`src/compactbench/runner/continuation.py:52-78`, user prompt drawn from an 8-item pool at line 19-28), so even a 5-cycle run stays under ~700 tokens.
  - *Why:* 471 tokens is 0.24% of a 200K window and 0.05% of a 1M window. No production system compacts at that length — compaction triggers at 50K-500K tokens. The benchmark therefore measures "can a summarizer retain 3 facts from a 40-turn toy dialogue", not "does a compaction method survive a real context-window overflow". A method that wins here tells you nothing about real traffic; the ranking is dominated by lexical retention behaviour that disappears at real scale, where the actual failure modes are attention dilution, positional recency bias, and hierarchical-summary information loss. This is the benchmark's largest external-validity risk and the honest answer to alexander-potemkin's "what to compare with?" is currently "nothing meaningful".
  - *Fix:* Add a real scale axis, not just more filler. (a) Introduce a `long_context` difficulty level with `distractor_turns: 2000-8000` and, critically, replace the canned-sentence distractor generator with realistic haystack material — sampled code files, doc paragraphs, tool output, and multi-turn side-threads — so a 50K-200K-token transcript is composed of genuinely varied text. (b) Add a `--target-context-tokens` knob to the engine that pads to a requested token budget rather than a turn count, so runs can be reported as "compaction at 50K / 200K / 500K". (c) Report scores per context-length bucket on the leaderboard; a method's score curve vs. input length is the interesting result. (d) Until this lands, rename the tier from `elite` to something honest and state the token range in `docs/methodology.md` so nobody over-reads the numbers.
- **[P0 · BUG · M]** A compactor that emits nothing scores 0.340 overall and 0.612 elite_score — 37% of items are free points
  - *Evidence:* `src/compactbench/scoring/checks.py:30-34` scores `forbidden_absent` as 1.0 whenever the forbidden string is not a substring of the response. Across the 24 public templates there are 63 evaluation items, of which 23 (36.5%) use `forbidden_absent`: 12 `planning_soundness` and 11 `forbidden_behavior_retention`. The forbidden values come from `_ACTION_PHRASES` (`src/compactbench/dsl/generators.py:137-163`) and are all famous anti-patterns — "store passwords in plaintext", "use regex to parse HTML", "call eval() on user input". No competent model proposes these unprompted, so the item passes whether or not the constraint survived compaction. I simulated a null compactor (empty artifact, honest "I don't have that information" response) and got per-template scores from 0.000 to 0.750, **mean 0.340**; `entity_confusion` family mean 0.425 and `decision_override` family mean 0.507 both clear the `MIN_FAMILY_MEAN_SCORE = 0.40` guard at `src/compactbench/leaderboard/qualification.py:14`. Its constraint_retention is 11/29 = 0.379 and its drift_resistance is 1.000 (stable), giving elite_score = 0.40(0.340) + 0.30(1.0) + 0.20(0.379) + 0.10(1.0) = **0.612** under `src/compactbench/leaderboard/ranking.py:46-48`. I confirmed this empirically: `compactbench run --method built-in:hybrid-ledger --suite starter` with the default mock provider (which returns `""`) reports overall_score 0.375, drift_resistance 1.000, planning_soundness mean 1.000 (8/8 perfect), forbidden_behavior_retention mean 0.667.
  - *Why:* The published elite_score is not anchored at zero. Every method gets ~0.61 for free, so the entire visible spread between a good and a bad method is compressed into the top ~0.39 of the scale — and readers will interpret 0.72 vs 0.68 as a meaningful gap when most of both numbers is the floor. Worse, the 0.40 family-diversity guard that is supposed to catch specialists is set *below* what nothing achieves on two of the four families.
  - *Fix:* Three fixes. (1) Publish a mandatory `null` and `identity` baseline: add `built-in:no-op` (returns an empty artifact) and `built-in:passthrough` compactors, run them, and pin their rows at the top of `docs/leaderboard.md` as calibration. This alone answers issue #38 today. (2) Make `forbidden_absent` items discriminative: pair every one with a `contains_normalized` on the *correct alternative*, so passing requires positively naming what to do instead, not merely omitting the anti-pattern. Concretely, in `benchmarks/public/*/decision_override_*.yaml`, change `reject_old` from bare `forbidden_absent` to a two-part check. (3) Normalize elite_score against the null baseline before publishing: `reported = (raw - null_baseline) / (1 - null_baseline)`.
- **[P0 · BUG · S]** An empty artifact reports the highest possible compression ratio and passes every tier floor
  - *Evidence:* `src/compactbench/scoring/compression.py:57-66` computes `src / max(dst, 1)`. When `artifact_tokens()` returns 0 the denominator is floored at 1, so the ratio becomes the full transcript token count. The docstring at line 62-64 acknowledges this ("the caller can detect this by checking that artifact_tokens is zero") but **no caller ever does** — `src/compactbench/scoring/scorer.py:82` stores it unconditionally, `src/compactbench/runner/persistence.py:264` means it into the run aggregate, and `src/compactbench/leaderboard/qualification.py:39` compares that value against `TIER_FLOORS`. My default-quickstart run above reported `compression_ratio 101.00x` for artifacts that were entirely empty. `compression_bonus` (`ranking.py:21-30`) caps at 1.0 at 2x the floor, so an empty artifact also maxes the 10% compression term.
  - *Why:* The metric that is supposed to stop degenerate methods rewards the most degenerate method possible. Combined with the free-points finding above, a method that returns `CompactionArtifact()` gets the best compression score on the board and clears the Elite-Aggressive floor by 12x.
  - *Fix:* In `src/compactbench/scoring/scorer.py:82`, guard the ratio: if `artifact_tokens(artifact) == 0`, set `compression_ratio = 0.0` and add a `degenerate_artifact: bool` field to `Scorecard`. In `qualification.py`, add an explicit reason `"artifact was empty in N cycles"` that disqualifies the run. Add a unit test in `tests/unit/test_scoring_compression.py` pinning the empty-artifact behaviour.
- **[P0 · BUG · M]** Variable generators never enforce distinctness — 16 of 24 templates produce logically unsatisfiable cases
  - *Evidence:* `src/compactbench/dsl/generators.py:236-243` resolves each variable independently via `derive_seed(base_seed, decl.name)` with no collision check, and `src/compactbench/dsl/validator.py` has no distinctness rule. I swept 500 seed slots per template and found same-generator collisions in 16 of 24 templates: `entity_confusion_v1_2` 16.6%, `decision_override_v1_2` 12.2%, `reference_resolution_v1_2` 8.2%, `entity_confusion_v1_3` 8.0%, `entity_confusion_v1_1`/`v1_5`/`starter` 7.0%, `decision_override_v1_3` 5.2%, down to 2.0%. Concrete degenerate case at slot 58 for `decision_override_v1_1`: `rejected_plan == approved_plan == 'trust the X-Forwarded-For header blindly'`. That template's `recall_decision` (weight 3) requires the phrase present and `reject_old` (weight 3) requires it absent — the maximum achievable score is 0.5, permanently.
